The chart's old defaults (128Mi request, 512Mi limit) were right for the quiet modules. Changed in the defaults: `sme` 480Mi with a 768Mi limit, `r1-termination` 192Mi. The measured CPU requests (gateway 500m, SME 250m, RAN NF OAM 150m, Postgres 250m) are in the opt-in profile `deploy/helm/smo/values-sized.yaml`: a first version put them in the defaults, which raised the chart's CPU requests from 1.3 to 2.2 cores and the kind upgrade job could no longer schedule Postgres (`Insufficient cpu`); a lab or trial install must still fit a two-core node, and `tests_integration/test_helm_sizing.py` keeps the default total at 1.5 cores or less. No CPU limits.
- **The first run's table was wrong in one way**: it counted the load generator's container (`friendly_dijkstra`, a core, 167 MiB) as part of the stack; `sizing_report.py` now keeps only containers named `smo-*`.
- **Not done.** A run with several replicas, with mTLS and tracing on, with the observability stack, and at a million alarms (`docs/SIZING.md`, "What this does not tell you"). The artifact of a run cannot be downloaded from the agent's environment, so the tables are read from the job log.

### PR-V-11b — a migration that cannot finish (V-11, second half)

- **Design.** The plan said "a test-only revision". Not done that way: a revision that fails on purpose would have to be in every image to be reached by `helm upgrade`, which is production code for a test. The failure an operator meets is a migration that cannot get its lock (a long transaction, a manual `LOCK`, a stuck session), so the lane holds `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` on `nfo.lcm_operation` (0027 adds a column to it) and gives the migrate Job a 60 s deadline. Both revisions after the previous release are written with `IF NOT EXISTS`, so a pre-created object would not have failed them.
- **What is asserted**, for the previous release only (the release before it cannot be rolled back to, and the extra time buys nothing): `helm upgrade` exits non-zero and the last revision is `failed`; the Deployments' generations are unchanged (the pre-upgrade hook failed before anything was applied); `alembic_version` is what it was and the new column and index do not exist (one transaction); the images are still the previous release's, every pod is ready, the previous release's `compose_e2e.py` passes and the audit chain verifies; then the unchanged upgrade command runs in the next step and passes.
- **Not done.** A revision whose own SQL fails after changing data (the transaction would undo it; not exercised); the full-chain downgrade with data. This lane has not run yet when this is written: the first run is the pull request's.

### PR-V-9b — the shared rate budget across replicas, under a burst (V-9, SEC-8.5)

- **What was missing.** `R1_RATE_STORE=postgres` (SEC-8.5) was proved at the statement level (`tests_integration/test_rate_limit_postgres.py`) and for its fail-open path; no run put several gateway processes behind one name and sent them a burst. The compose path (the variable, the role's grant on `rate_bucket`, the table from `0028`) was therefore untested.
- **The check.** `stress_run.py limiter-shared --budget per-replica|shared`: one burst of calls at once through Docker's DNS name of three replicas (`docker-compose.stress-shared.yml`, a budget of 20 and 5 a second). `shared_verdict` (unit tested in `tests_integration/test_stress_run.py`) says what the number let through means: with the shared store at most one budget plus the refill during the burst plus a slack of 8, and not far below it; for the control, more than that, or the check says it cannot tell the two apart. The workflow runs the control first (so the check is known to see the difference) and then recreates the gateway with `R1_RATE_STORE=postgres`; after each run it counts, per replica, the proxied requests in its log and requires at least two replicas to have answered five or more.
- **What the first run found.** With the shared store 43 of 120 calls were let through against a budget of about 30 (the control let 64 through, about three budgets). Cause (reproduced first as a unit test on SQLite, then fixed): `refilled_at = :now` let a statement stamped earlier than the row's clock move the bucket's clock back, so the next statement was refilled for the same interval twice; fixed with `GREATEST(refilled_at, :now)` (`shared/smo_shared/ratelimit.py`, tests on SQLite and on Postgres). A replica whose clock stays behind the others now adds no refill; the others' calls refill the bucket.
- **Not done.** More than one caller (the per-caller budget is the thing shared); a burst long enough to see the refill rate itself; a replica killed mid-burst. The first run is the pull request's, so the slack of 8 and the control's margin are unproved.

### PR-DB-2c — the compatibility views are dropped (revision 0029)

- **Why now.** `0023` to `0025` promised the views "until the revision after the next release": the next release after 0.3.0 is 0.4.0, which made them, so the revision after it is the first of 0.5.0. 0.4.0's modules use their roles' search paths (schema first, then `public`); the previous-release lane builds 0.4.0 from its tag and replays its runbook on the head schema, so it proves the rollback target does not need them.
- **How.** The upgrade reads the catalogue (a view in `public` that depends on a base table of the same name in another schema: what the three revisions made, nothing else) rather than a list copied from them. The downgrade recreates a view for every base table outside `public`, with the columns the table had when the view was made: a plain `SELECT *` at downgrade time included `lcm_operation.created_at`, which `0027` adds, and `0027`'s downgrade could not then drop the column (`DependentObjectsStillExist`); the full-chain downgrade to the baseline, run on a real Postgres, found it, and `ADDED_AFTER_THE_VIEWS` lists the one column concerned.
- **Tests.** `test_db_roles.py`: no view is left in `public`, the owner's bare name no longer resolves, and a role is refused another module's table by its real schema (the old check only ever saw the view); `test_migrations.py` pins the head and runs every revision up and down on Postgres.
- **The upgrade-from-the-previous-release check named tables with no schema** (`scripts/check_upgrade_from_previous_release.sh`: the package and instance snapshot, and the "seeded break" control, which does `ALTER TABLE application_package ADD COLUMN ... NOT NULL`). The snapshot failed once the views were gone (`relation "application_package" does not exist`). The control was worse than failing: while the name resolved to the compatibility view, Postgres refused the `ALTER` because it does not apply to views (`ALTER action ADD COLUMN cannot be performed ... not supported for views`), so "a migration that cannot run on a populated database was refused" was true for a reason that had nothing to do with the data. Both now name `onboarding.application_package` and `rapp_mgmt.rapp_instance`, and the control is refused for the real reason (`column "seeded_break" ... contains null values`, checked on Postgres 16).
- **Not done, deliberately.** The A1 tables and `smo_a1_related`: 0.4.0's code still uses them, and the rollback to 0.4.0 is promised, so they are dropped by the revision after the next release. The upgrade lane's rollback step proves 0.4.0 runs on this schema.

### PR-V-6b — the upgrade over data, timed per revision

- **What was missing.** `docs/VALIDATION.md` listed "the migration of a large table is untimed". The volume lane migrated an empty database to head and seeded it afterwards; the upgrade lane on kind upgrades a database with a runbook's worth of rows. Neither said what `0027` (an index on half a million performance files) costs.
- **How.** `scripts/upgrade_at_volume.py` lists the revisions after a given one from `migrations/versions`, runs `scripts/migrate.py --revision <id>` for each, times it, and fails on a failed revision or a total over the budget. The workflow reads the previous release's last revision from the newest non-rc `smo-v*` tag (so it needs the tags: `fetch-depth: 0`), migrates a fresh database to it, seeds it (`load_seed.py` now takes `--database`), and runs the script. Each row includes the process start of `migrate.py`; the table says so.
- **Not done.** Lock waits behind live traffic; the pods rolling; a revision that rewrites a table with a volatile default (none exists yet).

### PR-DB-3 — retention (DB-3.1 to 3.7)

Closed from `OPEN_ITEMS.md`. `docs/RETENTION.md` is the table (DB-3.1): each high-volume table, what grows it, its setting, a proposed value and what removes the rows. Every setting is a number of days with `0` (or unset, or not a number) meaning keep, so an upgrade deletes nothing; the proposed values (alarms 90, PM files 14, refusals 90, MDAF reports 30, GUI audit log 365) are for the operator to confirm and are not defaults. `smo_shared/retention.py` has `retention_days(variable)` and `purge(db, model, column, days, where)`: it selects up to 1000 primary keys older than the cutoff (with the extra conditions), deletes them, commits, and repeats, so a purge is many small transactions and may run again after a crash; a NULL date never compares older, so a row with no date stays; it refuses `days <= 0` and a table without a single-column key. The purges are tasks of the module's own worker, so they run as the module's database role (PR-DB-2) and at most once an hour across all workers (PR-MSG-4): RAN NF OAM `purge-cleared-alarms` (`alarm.cleared_at`, so an alarm that is still raised is never deleted) and `purge-pm-files` (`pm_file.file_ready_time`; the content is a column, so there is no file on disk to remove, which closes that half of DB-3.4), next to the existing `purge-safeguard-refusals`; and a new `mdaf-worker` (compose, chart, mTLS override and certificate) with `purge-reports`. The GUI BFF is not on `smo_shared` and has no worker, so its audit log is purged by `python -m app.retention` from cron or a CronJob: it exports the batch to `gui-audit-<date>-<last id>.jsonl` first when `GUI_AUDIT_EXPORT_DIR` is set, deletes only the ids it exported, and does so with a bulk `DELETE` because the ORM guard refuses any change to the log. Not taken: pruning the platform audit chain (`audit_log` is hash-chained, so removing the oldest rows breaks `verify` until a signed checkpoint exists), and removing `DEAD` outbox rows (they are what an operator looks at). Tests: `shared/tests/test_retention.py`, `ran-nf-oam/tests/test_tasks.py`, `mdaf/tests/test_tasks.py`, `gui-bff/tests/test_retention.py`; the purges are not tested against Postgres at volume.

**DB-3.10 (0.6.0) — samples and a warning.** Decided: the code and chart defaults stay `0` (an upgrade deletes nothing); what ships is the proposals and a signal. `deploy/helm/smo/values-production.yaml` (next to `values-sized.yaml`) sets the five periods of `docs/RETENTION.md` in `modules.ran-nf-oam-worker.env`, `ran-nf-oam.env` (for `POST /safeguard-refusals/purge`), `mdaf-worker.env` and `gui.env`; the `prod` GitOps overlay carries the same numbers; `.env.example` lists them commented out (compose already passed the worker variables through; it now passes `GUI_AUDIT_*` to gui-bff too). The chart defaults gained `SAFEGUARD_REFUSAL_RETENTION_DAYS: "0"` on `ran-nf-oam` and its worker so that overlays may set it. **No CronJob for the GUI audit purge (deliberately not taken):** gui-bff's SQLite file sits on a ReadWriteOnce volume held by one pod under `strategy: Recreate`; a CronJob pod would need the same claim, which only schedules on the same node and would write the SQLite file from a second process. The documented way is `kubectl exec deploy/gui-bff -- python -m app.retention` from the operator's scheduler, with the two variables in `gui.env`. **Warning and metric:** `smo_shared.retention.report_retention_off(db, table, model, days)`, called by the purge tasks `purge-cleared-alarms`, `purge-pm-files`, `purge-safeguard-refusals` (RAN NF OAM) and `purge-reports` (MDAF) on every run: with `days > 0` it drops the series, with `0` it estimates the rows (`pg_class.reltuples` through `to_regclass`, `-1` read as 0; `count(*)` on other databases), sets `smo_retention_off_rows{table}` (`metrics.record_retention_off_rows`, a collector like the worker-success one) and logs one WARNING per table per UTC day above `SMO_RETENTION_WARN_ROWS` (default 1000000, `0` never); it never raises. `gui-bff/app/retention.py` has its own count and warning (no gauge) because the BFF does not install `smo_shared`. Not taken: an alert rule (a kept table is a decision, not a fault; an alert would need a runbook and an SLO-document entry for something that is not a failure). Tests: `tests_integration/test_retention_samples.py` (the document's table, the Helm sample, the GitOps overlay, `.env.example` and compose agree; defaults keep everything; no CronJob; the rendered checks run where `helm` exists), the unit tests above, and the metric name and label in `tests_integration/test_alert_rules.py`.

### PR-SB-9a — the O1 adaptor conformance kit, configuration and discovery (SB-9.1, 9.2, 9.6, 9.7)

Closed from `OPEN_ITEMS.md`; the fault, performance, software and heartbeat checks (9.3 to 9.5) stay open as `SB-9.8` because they run the other way. `conformance/o1` is a standalone package: `python -m conformance.o1 --adaptor URL [--protocol auto|netconf|restconf|both] [--out report]` runs a registry of checks over HTTP and writes `report.json` and `report.md`; exit 0 when every check that ran passed, 1 when one failed. The checks are the contract RAN NF OAM's own clients use (`netconf_client.py`: an `<rpc><edit-config>` or `<get-config>` posted as XML to the adaptor's URI; `restconf_client.py`: data resources under a RESTCONF root; the vendor discovery of `GET /capabilities`), 24 of them: discovery (shape, vocabulary, a transport that is run is declared), NETCONF-shaped (merge, read back, merge keeps the rest, a function addressed apart from its element, delete, an empty merge and a missing ref refused, unparseable XML answered without a 5xx, the message-id echoed, special characters escaped, an entity not expanded) and RESTCONF-shaped (host-meta, PUT 201 then 204, read back, PATCH merges, PUT replaces, a function below its element, DELETE 204 then 404, a mismatched key and a body that is not a list entry refused with 400 and an `ietf-restconf:errors` body, an empty PATCH refused). Every write is read back, because an agent that acknowledges a write and keeps nothing is the commonest real defect. Each run uses references new to it, so a second run against the same adaptor passes. Decision: the kit tests what the SMO uses, not RFC 6241 or 8040 as a whole, and says so in `conformance/README.md`, which lists every check (a test keeps the table in step with the registry). It is tested against the mock in process, against fakes that each break one thing (an adaptor that acknowledges everything, one that answers 500, one that expands an entity, one with no declaration), and against the mock with an `IGNORE_WRITE` fault injected (NC-2 and RC-3 fail); in CI (`o1-conformance` in `smo-tests.yml`) it runs twice over real HTTP against the mock as a process and keeps the report. `mock-o1-adaptor` is the RAN O1 stub of this repository for configuration; the emitting side (alarms, PM files, heartbeat from the stub) is not built.

### PR-GUI-8a — the operator page a rApp declares: the decision, the check at onboarding and the SDK helper (GUI-8.1, 8.2, 8.8)

Closed from `OPEN_ITEMS.md`; the GUI backend, the directory and detail pages, the renderer, the pins and the migration of the four samples (GUI-8.3 to 8.6) were the next stage (PR-GUI-8b below); only the browser check, GUI-8.7, is still open. The record is `docs/adr/0004-operator-ui-declaration.md`; the format is `smo_shared/operator_ui.py`.

- **The declaration (GUI-8.1).** A rApp package may carry `operatorUi` in `manifest.yaml` (under `rappManifest` or at the top level, like `limits`): `version` (1), `readOnly`, and 1 to 20 ordered `panels` of kind `table`, `keyValues`, `kpis`, `chart` or `actions`. Every panel has `id`, `title` and (except a KPI panel bound only to KPI names, and an actions panel) a `source`: a GET route relative to the rApp's operator API, with `{instanceId}` bound to the open instance, an optional fixed `query` and `refreshSeconds` (5 to 3600). Fields are picked with a path subset: dotted names and at most one `[]`. A table has columns with a format (`text`, `number`, `percent`, `datetime`, `badge`, `id`, `boolean`, `list`, `sparkline`) and row actions that may use `{row.<field>}` and show only for some rows (`when`). An action has a method (POST, PUT, PATCH, DELETE), a route, a `success` text, an optional `confirm`, typed `inputs` (string, integer, number, boolean, enum) and fixed `body` values, where `"{user}"` is filled by the GUI backend with the signed-in user. Text is always rendered as text, never HTML or markdown. Limits: 64 KiB as JSON, 4 000 values, 20 panels, 20 columns, 5 row actions, 10 actions per panel, 8 inputs. `x-` keys are ignored and dropped; any other unknown key, kind or version is refused at onboarding, while the GUI draws a kind it does not know as "unsupported panel".
- **`rowDetail` (added to version 1 by the owner's decision, before anything was released).** The first draft left per-row drawers out as a proposed version 2; the owner decided to build them before the four sample pages are migrated, so they are part of version 1 and `version` stays 1. An optional field of a `table`: a `title` (may use `{row.<field>}`) and 1 to 6 blocks, each `json` (the row or a sub-object by path, formatted text, with an `empty` text), `keyValues` (picked fields of the row), `table` (a list field of the row, or a per-row GET `source`; columns as for a table, `sparkline` allowed) or `chart` (a list field of the row, or a per-row `source`). A per-row `source` may bind `{row.<field>}` in its route and, as a whole value, in a query parameter. Every `{row.<field>}` (detail source, title, and also row-action routes) must name the table's `rowKey` or a column path. No nesting; row buttons stay in the table. The per-row sources are declared routes (`declared_routes`, `route_allowed`; reads need viewer). An unknown block kind is refused at onboarding and drawn as "unsupported block" by the GUI. The values and byte limits count the blocks. With it all four sample drawers are expressible; what still cannot be: text composed from several fields, a map's entries, threshold colours and chart presentation, a second drawer level (ADR, last section). The Energy Saving example now declares its cell drawer (chart, latest decision as JSON, the last 20 decisions of the cell) in place of a separate history panel.
- **Where it lives.** In `manifest.yaml`, not in a separate `ui/operator-ui.json`: Onboarding already parses the manifest and stores its AI part as `aiCapabilities`, which rApp Management and AIMgF already read from `onboarding-status`, so there is no new file, column, migration or OpenAPI change (the declaration is `aiCapabilities.operatorUi`). A separate file can be allowed later (`operatorUi: {file: ...}`) without breaking a package.
- **How the rApp's operator API is reached.** (a) was chosen over (b): the instance registers an `operatorApiBase` in rApp Management and the GUI backend calls the declared routes through the R1 gateway, which resolves a dynamic prefix to that base. (b), static gateway routes (today's `/energy-saving-rapp` and so on), gives a rApp onboarded at run time no route until the gateway is changed. No decisive problem with (a): the base is supplied by a workload, so it is accepted only from the instance's own authenticated call or an operator, must be http(s) and pass `is_safe_webhook_destination` when stored and before each call, the same guard every caller-supplied destination has. Built in PR-GUI-8b.
- **Permission.** The allowed `(method, path-template)` set is exactly the union of the panels' `source` routes and the action routes (`declared_routes`); `route_allowed` matches a concrete path against it and refuses `..`, `%`, `//`, `?`, `#` and any segment outside `A-Za-z0-9._~-`. Reads need viewer, changes operator, an undeclared route is refused, a `readOnly` rApp allows no change (and Onboarding refuses a `readOnly` declaration that declares an action), every change is audited. These functions are written and tested here; the GUI backend uses them (PR-GUI-8b).
- **Validation (GUI-8.2).** `validate_operator_ui` is called by `_parse_ai_capabilities` and raises `OperatorUiInvalid` with the place and the rule (`operatorUi.panels[2].columns[1].path: must not contain '..'`); Onboarding turns it into `PackageValidationFailed`, so the package is `FAILED` as for any validation failure. The message is logged and returned as `failureReason` in the `202` answer of `POST /packages` (also for the other failures: the exception name, no internals). It is not stored: that would need a column and a migration, and a package that failed is onboarded again once fixed; if operators need to read it later, a nullable `failure_reason` is an additive revision. The check counts values iteratively before it serialises anything, so a YAML alias bomb is refused without expanding it. Refused: unknown kind, key or version; a source that is not a GET; an action that is a GET; `..`, `%`, empty segments, `?`, `#` or a foreign `{param}` in a route; a field path outside the subset; every limit above; duplicate panel ids and action ids; inputs and fixed values of one name; `readOnly` with an action; control characters; non-JSON values. A package without `operatorUi` is unchanged: the four sample packages are not touched and their committed `.csar` files still match their sources. There is no list of a rApp's API routes in the manifest to check an action against, so none is checked: the declared routes are the surface. The schema is `docs/schemas/operator-ui-1.schema.json`, generated from the same constants (a test fails if it drifts); the worked example is `docs/schemas/operator-ui.energy-saving.example.yaml`, which the ADR embeds, the shared and Onboarding tests load, and the JSON Schema accepts.
- **SDK (GUI-8.8).** `smo_sdk.operator_ui`: builder functions (`declaration`, `table`, `key_values`, `kpis`, `chart`, `actions`, `action`, `column`, `item`, `tile`, `input_field`, `source`) that validate with the same code, `to_yaml`, and `add_to_manifest` (appends the block to a `manifest.yaml`, keeping comments, refusing a second declaration). `sdk/examples/hello_operator_ui.py` is a new minimal package (a CSAR with no code) built with it; the four sample rApps are not changed. `samples/build_csar.py` zips a directory and has no manifest assembly step, so the helper writes the manifest text and the sample CSAR build is not touched.
- **What the four pages need that the declaration cannot express** (ADR, last section): per-row sparklines were added to version 1 as a column format; row drawers are covered by `rowDetail` (above); a map's entries (`datasets`), composed text and threshold colours stay out.
- **Not taken.** A rApp shipping JavaScript or an iframe (code in the operator's session with the operator's rights; after 1.0.0 if ever). Persisting the failure reason (a column, for no use before an operator asks). A check of an action route against an API description (the manifest has none).
- **Tests.** `shared/tests/test_operator_ui.py` (75: the example accepted and valid against the JSON Schema, every rejection above, extension keys, the alias bomb, the allowed-route match with traversal and encoded-slash attempts, the schema file equal to the code's, the ADR embedding the example); `onboarding/tests/test_main.py` (27 more: the example onboards and is stored, 6 `rowDetail` refusals and the stored drawer, 14 refusals with the place and rule in `failureReason`, no `operatorUi` unchanged, the log line); `sdk/tests/test_operator_ui.py` (12: builders, the writer, the example package).

### PR-GUI-8b — the rApp directory, the declared page, pins, and the four sample rApps moved to declarations (GUI-8.3 to 8.7)

Closed from `OPEN_ITEMS.md`: with this the whole of `PR-GUI-8` is built, except the browser check of GUI-8.7 against the compose stack, which could not be run where the change was made (below). PR-GUI-8a fixed the format and the check at onboarding (`docs/adr/0004-operator-ui-declaration.md`); this is what draws it, what permits it and what ships it.

- **Where a rApp's operator API is (GUI-8.3, ADR 4, choice (a)).** `rapp_instance.operator_api_base` (revision `0030`, `ALTER TABLE rapp_mgmt.rapp_instance ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS operator_api_base TEXT`; nullable, expand only: the previous release neither reads nor writes it, and every row already there is "none registered"). rApp Management takes it at create (`operatorApiBase` in `POST /instances`, an operator's choice) or later from `PUT /instances/{id}/operator-api` (and `DELETE`, idempotent; `GET` answers `{instanceId, state, operatorApiBase}`, null for a terminated instance). `smo_shared.webhook.normalise_base_url` is the check, run when it is stored (422 `OPERATOR_API_BASE_INVALID`) and again by the gateway before every call: http or https only, at most 300 characters, no space or control character, no user information, query or fragment, no `..`, `%`, `//` or backslash in the path, port 0 refused, and `is_safe_webhook_destination` (no loopback, link-local or metadata address, no `localhost`); a trailing slash is dropped. Who may register: an internal caller (the operator's GUI, a module) for any instance, a caller with the rApp role only for its own (`X-R1-Invoker-Id` must be the instance's `oauthClientId`, else 403 `NOT_THIS_INSTANCE`); `/rapp-mgmt` `PUT|DELETE /instances/{id}/operator-api` is on the rApp change allow-list (`smo_shared/roles.py`) for that. A terminated instance refuses a registration (409). `operatorApiBase` is also in `GET /instances` and `GET /instances/{id}` for the GUI backend; the browser is only told whether one is registered. The four sample rApps run in compose under their own identity (one container for any number of instances), not the instance's, so they cannot register themselves; the demo scripts and the integration environments pass `operatorApiBase` when they create the instance.
- **The gateway's dynamic prefix (GUI-8.3).** `/rapps/{instanceId}/operator/<route>` is resolved by `r1-termination/app/operator_api.py` to the instance's base (rApp Management `GET /instances/{id}/operator-api`, asked as an internal caller; cached per instance for `R1_OPERATOR_API_CACHE_SECONDS`, default 5, `0` asks every time; when rApp Management cannot answer, an answer under a minute old is used, else 503 `OPERATOR_API_UNRESOLVED` with `Retry-After`; none registered or unknown: 404 `OPERATOR_API_NOT_REGISTERED`). Everything before the forward is the existing path: token introspection, the rate limit, the dot-segment refusal, the role policy, the kill switch and the audit of changes. The route after `operator/` may hold only segments of letters, digits and `._~-` (404 `NO_ROUTE` otherwise). The forward goes through `smo_shared.webhook.forward_to_destination`, a new helper beside `post_webhook`/`get_webhook`/`delete_webhook` for the one thing they cannot do (any method, headers, query and body, an async client): it refuses a destination that fails the guard (502, no reason given), applies the mTLS context to an https destination inside the deployment, counts the call under the target `operator-api`, and lets a timeout (504) or other transport error (502) surface as a fixed body, never the exception text or the rApp's address. It is not a notification (nothing is stored or retried), so it is not a row of `docs/NOTIFICATIONS.md`'s inventory, which lists the other three helpers and `enqueue`; the file says so. **What reaches the rApp:** content type, accept, language, user agent, the correlation and trace ids, and the identity the gateway vouches for (`X-R1-Role`, `X-R1-Invoker-Id`, `X-R1-On-Behalf-Of` from an internal caller). The `Authorization` header and cookies are never forwarded: the base is a URL a workload registered, and the BFF's SMO token must not reach it. The rApp's `Set-Cookie` and hop-by-hop headers are dropped on the way back. **Who may call:** reads are open to every valid token like every module's, and the sample rApps use that for their peer coordination (below); a change by an rApp is refused as everywhere (`/rapps` is not in `RAPP_MAY_CHANGE`). The four static routes (`/energy-saving-rapp`, `/mobility-optimization-rapp`, `/coverage-optimization-rapp`, `/traffic-steering-rapp`) and their `*_RAPP_URL` variables are gone.
- **The GUI backend (GUI-8.3, `gui-bff/app/rapps.py`, `operator_ui.py`).** `GET /api/rapps` (the directory: instances joined with their package's name, version and vendor, and whether the package declares a page and the instance an operator API; `search` over name, version, owner and ids, `state`, `owner`, `hasPage`, `pinned`; paged, stable order; the owners and states present are returned for the filters), `GET /api/rapps/{instance}` (the instance, its declaration read from Onboarding's `onboarding-status`, `declarationState` `declared`, `none` or `unreadable`, and `canChange`: the user is an operator and the declaration is not `readOnly`), and `ANY /api/rapps/{instance}/operator/{route}`. The browser never learns the rApp's address. The BFF does not install `smo_shared`, so `declared_routes`, `required_role`, `_template_regex` and `route_allowed` are a **vendored copy** at the top of `gui-bff/app/operator_ui.py`; `tests_integration/test_gui_bff_operator_ui_parity.py` compares each definition with the original node by node, runs both on three declarations and about 20 000 method and path pairs (traversals, encoded slashes, backslashes, unicode, dot segments), and checks that `decide` never allows what the original refuses; the BFF's own `tests/test_operator_ui.py` repeats the cases of `shared/tests/test_operator_ui.py` that concern the matcher so the BFF job needs no shared code. `decide` answers in this order: a declaration that is not the validated shape, 403 `UNDECLARED_ROUTE` (it never raises); a change on a `readOnly` rApp, 403 `RAPP_READ_ONLY` (before the route check: an undeclared change on such a rApp says why); a route not declared **for this instance** (`{instanceId}` is bound to the page that is open, so another rApp's id in a declared template is refused), 403 `UNDECLARED_ROUTE` whatever the role; the role (viewer reads, operator changes), 403 `FORBIDDEN`; then the query of a read (only what the source declares: a fixed value is the declared one whatever the browser sent, a whole-value `{row.<field>}` takes the browser's value and must be one safe segment, else 422 `INVALID_QUERY`) or the body of a change (only the declared inputs, checked for type and bounds, then the fixed `body` with `"{user}"` replaced by the session user; a field the browser added is dropped, a fixed value cannot be overridden, a missing required input is 422 `INVALID_INPUT`, not an object 400, over 64 KiB 413; a DELETE sends no body). Two actions on one route are told apart by the `X-Action-Id` header the GUI sends; without it the first is taken. **Audit:** every refusal is a `DENIED` row; every change is two `RAPP_ACTION` rows, `phase=requested` before the call is sent and `phase=done` with the status after it (also when the SMO could not be reached: 502 and the title, never the exception), each naming the rApp instance and the action id. The declaration and the package index are cached in the BFF process for 60 and 30 seconds (a package id is immutable, an upgrade is a new one). Pins: `GET /api/me/pins` (with names; a pin of an instance that no longer exists is dropped when read, and only when rApp Management answered), `PUT` (idempotent; 409 `PIN_LIMIT` at five; the instance must exist) and `DELETE /api/me/pins/{instance}`, in the new table `gui_rapp_pin` of the BFF's own store (`create_all`; no column was added, so `_add_missing_columns` is unchanged); of two pins at once that would pass five, the one that finds itself over takes its row back; deleting a user removes the pins. The static rules and `MODULES` entries of the four sample rApps are gone from `rbac.py` (they were `ENERGY_SAVING…` operator and admin rules); `/api/modules/status` therefore probes 16 modules (R1 and 15), not 20.
- **The GUI (GUI-8.4, 8.5).** `lib/operatorUi.ts` (pure: field paths with at most one `[]` that only follow names the object itself has, route and query filling that refuses a value that is not one safe segment, `when`, formats, chart series with at most eight drawn, input checking, refresh interval clamped to 5 to 3600 s) and `components/OperatorUi.tsx` (the renderer). Panels: `table` (rows picked by `rows`, `rowKey`, columns with the nine formats, row actions with `when`, a click opens the drawer), `keyValues`, `kpis` (a `path` tile reads the source, a `kpi` tile the latest performance report of the instance, so a page of only `kpi` tiles needs no operator API), `chart` (SVG, line or bar, a legend per `seriesBy` value), `actions`. `rowDetail` blocks: `json` (with the `empty` text), `keyValues`, `table` and `chart` (from a list field of the row, or fetched for the open row when the drawer opens, with `{row.<field>}` bound to that row only). A button asks for its `inputs` in a dialog (checked before sending), asks `confirm` with the browser's dialog, sends the action id, and toasts the declared `success` text or the error. A change button is drawn only when the BFF says `canChange` (so a viewer sees none and a sentence saying why). An unknown panel kind, an unknown column shape or a chart of an unknown type is a card saying "unsupported panel"; an unknown block is "unsupported block"; an unknown format is text; a panel that throws is "could not be drawn" and the others are drawn (an error boundary per panel and per block). A rApp with no registered operator API gets a notice and its source panels are not requested. Text is always a text node: a title of `<script>` and a value of `<img onerror>` are drawn as characters, a badge's colour comes from the GUI's table of state words. `refreshSeconds` polls the source at its interval; without it a panel is read when it opens and by its refresh button. The pages: the directory is the first tab of `/rapps` (Packages and Instances remain the other two; search after a 250 ms pause, state, owner and page filters, a pinned-only filter, paging by 25, a star to pin), `/rapps/<instance>` shows the declared panels and under them the platform overview every rApp has (lifecycle with its buttons, KPIs reported, safeguards, faults, version history), and the sidebar lists the user's pins under the one "rApps" entry. The Deploy dialog has an optional "Operator API base URL". The four pages, their four sidebar entries, routes and the Es/Mro/Cco/Ts types are deleted.
- **The four sample rApps (GUI-8.6).** Each manifest carries its `operatorUi` (Energy Saving: the ADR's worked example, verbatim; the other three built with `smo_sdk.operator_ui`, with the defaults the validator fills in left out): an instance block, the two buttons (Evaluate now, Reconcile approvals), and the cells or relations table with sparklines and a drawer (trend chart, the latest execution as JSON, the last 20 decisions of the row; Coverage has three charts, one per share). `samples/<name>/tests/test_operator_page.py` (new, one per rApp) checks that the declaration validates, that every declared route is served by the rApp's own FastAPI app, that every declared query parameter is taken by its route, and that every declared field is in the answer the rApp really gives after an evaluation (a typo in a path fails), with the Energy Saving declaration also equal to the ADR example. The peer coordination of Mobility, Coverage and Traffic Steering (they read Energy Saving's cells and the others' relations over R1) moved from the static routes to `/rapps/{instanceId}/operator/...` (`_peer`); a peer that registered no operator API answers 404, read as "publishes nothing", as an unreachable one was. The CSARs are rebuilt. `tests_integration/mesh.py` resolves the dynamic prefix the way the gateway does (through rApp Management), and the four integration environments and the four `demo.py` pass `operatorApiBase` when they create the instance.
- **What the declared pages lose against the coded ones** (ADR, last section; each is a field the rApp could add to its answer): text composed from several fields (`source → target` in a cell, `fromCio → toCio`, the actuator line, the model line `id vN (artifact M)`, Coverage's tilt and power as `3.0° · 43 dBm`); the `datasets` map; the Coverage page's "Latest joint plan" card (the plan lives inside one cell's decision); the one-cell safety/intent/action/verification summary cells (the row's drawer shows the whole execution as JSON); threshold colours and fixed chart sizes. Start and lifecycle (`train`, `validate`, `emulate`, `deploy`) and the simulator producer were never buttons on these pages and are not declared, so the GUI backend refuses them; they remain API calls.
- **Not taken.** A rApp shipping JavaScript or an iframe (ADR). Enforcing `when` in the BFF (it is a presentation rule on the row the browser holds; the permission is the route and the role). Checking that a `{row.<field>}` value in a call is the value of a real row (the BFF cannot know the rows; it enforces that the value fills only the places the declaration names and is one safe segment, and the rApp's own checks apply to the rest). Showing the state of an `actions` panel's `source` (the field exists in the format; the renderer ignores it). Letting a sample rApp register its own operator API in compose (it does not run with the instance's identity). A chart library (a 150-line SVG component covers line and bar; a richer chart is a new format version).
- **Browser check (GUI-8.7; built later: PR-GUI-8c): the compose-stack job is not written; a manual pass was made against a fake SMO.** `smo-gui-e2e` needs Docker and Chromium, and neither was usable where this was written (no Docker daemon, no Playwright browser download), so a script that could not be run was not added to the job. What was done instead, once and by hand: the built GUI (`vite preview`), the real BFF (uvicorn on SQLite) and a throwaway fake of R1, SME, rApp Management, Onboarding and the gateway's operator prefix, whose stub rApps answered with the shape of the sample dashboards and whose packages carried the real `operatorUi` of the Energy Saving and Mobility manifests; driven by headless Chromium 131 (`@sparticuz/chromium` with `playwright-core`). Results: a rApp added to the fake SMO after the GUI was built appeared in the directory and had its declared page (panels, sparklines, the row drawer with its chart, JSON and a history table fetched for the open row); a viewer had no change button and was told why, and a hand-made change was refused (`FORBIDDEN`), as were an undeclared route and another instance's id (`UNDECLARED_ROUTE`); the rApp's address was in no answer the browser got; an operator's click on a row action reached the rApp with the session user filled in and left the two `RAPP_ACTION` audit rows; a value of `low load <b>1</b>` was drawn as text; a pin showed in the sidebar and survived a reload; `axe-core` (WCAG 2 A and AA) found no serious or critical violation on the directory, a declared page and a row drawer, after it had found `scrollable-region-focusable` on the drawer (and on scrollable tables and `pre` blocks in it), which got `tabIndex={0}` and a focus outline (the same markup was on every existing drawer; the browser check had never opened one). One full run of the harness timed out on a click while Playwright waited for the navigation it started; six repeats of the same click and the later full runs did not, and it was not explained. The harness is not in the repository. Still open as `GUI-8.7` in `OPEN_ITEMS.md`: the same flow in `smo-gui-e2e` against the compose stack, with a package onboarded through Onboarding and an instance deployed through rApp Management (a stub rApp on the compose network is enough).
- **Tests.** `rapp-mgmt/tests/test_operator_api.py` (24), `r1-termination/tests/test_operator_api.py` (30), `shared/tests/test_webhook_forward.py` (38), `gui-bff/tests/test_operator_ui.py` (40) and `test_rapps.py` (36), `tests_integration/test_gui_bff_operator_ui_parity.py` (12), the four `samples/*/tests/test_operator_page.py` (17), and in the GUI `lib/operatorUi.test.ts` (24), `components/OperatorUi.test.tsx` (28) and `pages/RappPages.test.tsx` (14). The contract tests of rApp Management and R1 Termination cover the new routes.

### PR-SB-9b — the O1 conformance kit's emitting side, and an emitting stub (SB-9.3, 9.4, 9.5, 9.8)

Closed from `OPEN_ITEMS.md`; with this the whole of PR-SB-9 is done. PR-SB-9a checked the half of the contract where RAN NF OAM calls the adaptor. The adaptor also *emits* (alarms, PM reports and files, software-management phase results, heartbeats), and neither the kit nor the stub could do that, because there is no standard way to ask an adaptor to emit. Decision: the kit defines a small **trigger API** that the adaptor under test provides, and `mock-o1-adaptor` implements it.

*The stub.* Five `POST` routes in `mock-o1-adaptor/app/main.py`, each with request and response models in its OpenAPI document: `/emit/alarm` (`managedElementRef`, `severity`, `sourceAlarmId` defaulting to a new UUID, `probableCause`, `specificProblem`, `managedFunctionRef`, `alarmType`, `correlationGroup`) posts RAN NF OAM's `POST /alarms/ingest`, whose fields are *query parameters* there (unset ones are left out); `/emit/pm-report` posts `POST /pm-reports` and `/emit/pm-file` posts `POST /pm-files` (the `PmReportRequest` / `PmFileRequest` bodies); `/emit/heartbeat` posts `/o1-adaptor-endpoints/{id}/heartbeat`; `/emit/software-phase` posts `/software-management-jobs/{id}/advance?succeeded=` (ids in a path are percent-encoded). The target is the new `MOCK_O1_OAM_URL` (compose and the chart set `http://ran-nf-oam:8000`, mirrored so `test_helm_chart.py` agrees) or a `target` in the request, which must pass `smo_shared.webhook.is_safe_webhook_destination` (an http(s) origin that is not a literal loopback or link-local address; 422 otherwise). The answer is `{emitted, status, response, target}`: `emitted` is RAN NF OAM answering 2xx; a refusal (an unknown severity, a service the element does not offer, no PM subscription) is **relayed** with `emitted: false` and RAN NF OAM's status and body, never a 500; RAN NF OAM unreachable (connect error, 5 s timeout) is a 502 and no target configured a 409, both `application/problem+json` bodies. The routes read the same `MOCK_O1_SUPPORTED_SERVICES` as `GET /capabilities`: `FM` (alarm), `PM` (pm-report), `FILE` (pm-file), `SWM` (software-phase), `HEARTBEAT` (heartbeat); a route for a service the stub does not declare answers 409. The call is a plain `httpx.post`, so the in-process service mesh of `tests_integration` reaches the real RAN NF OAM app with it.

*The kit.* `--oam-url` (RAN NF OAM, read back and prepared), `--emit-url` (the trigger API; default `--adaptor`), `--emit-prefix` (default `/emit`), `--element` (use an element that is already registered). Twelve checks in four groups, ids `FM-1..4`, `PM-1..3`, `SW-1..3`, `HB-1..2` (`conformance/o1/emit_checks.py`; the README table is kept in step with the registry by the existing test). A check now carries the MnS service it needs (`Check.service`); `kit.run` skips it, saying why, when the adaptor does not declare that service in `supportedServices` and when `--oam-url` is missing, as it skips a CM protocol that is not part of the run. The kit prepares what RAN NF OAM needs, once per run: a uniquely named element registered through `POST /o1-adaptor-endpoints` with the services the adaptor declares (so `require_service` has an answer), a PM subscription on a counter new to the run, a software-management job per SW check. FM: the ingest answers 200 with an alarmId; `GET /alarms` lists the alarm with the cause, problem, type and correlation group sent; severity in any case is listed lower case with the upper-case `perceivedSeverity`; two alarms with the same source id get two different UUIDs that are not the source id. PM: a report on a subscribed counter is 201 and counts its measurements (it is not stored, it goes to DME, so RAN NF OAM's answer is all there is to read); a file is 201, listed by `GET /files` with format and size, and the stored file holds the element, the counter and the cells. SW: DOWNLOAD done reads back as INSTALL / IN_PROGRESS, three results end COMPLETED, a failed phase ends FAILED. HB: `lastHeartbeatAt` moves and the endpoint is ACTIVE (a DISCOVERED one becomes ACTIVE), twice. Each check reads RAN NF OAM's state; none counts a 2xx.

*Tests.* `mock-o1-adaptor/tests/test_emit.py` (24 cases: each URL, query and body; relayed refusal; non-JSON answer; connect error and timeout as 502; no target; a request `target` and the refused ones; a service not declared, all five, and absent from `/capabilities`; path-id encoding; the OpenAPI responses). `tests_integration/test_o1_conformance_emit.py` runs the stub wired to the real RAN NF OAM app and the DME behind it through the mesh: every emitting check passes (and the whole kit, twice, both protocols); the registered endpoint carries the declared services and a group the stub does not declare is skipped; no `--oam-url` skips with the reason; `--element`; the runner's options; and fakes that each break one thing (says `emitted` and sends nothing: FM-2..4, PM-2, PM-3, SW-1..3, HB-1, HB-2 fail; sends the wrong severity: only FM-3 fails; never reports the software phase: only SW-1..3 fail; no trigger API: every emitting check fails naming the 404; a refusal from RAN NF OAM names what it said). In CI the compose end-to-end job runs the kit against the stack after the runbook replay (the replay expects the stack untouched; the kit leaves an element, alarms and files behind), from a Python container on the compose network, with none skipped; the process-based job `o1-conformance` is unchanged and runs the CM groups only (RAN NF OAM needs Postgres-shaped infrastructure and a DME behind R1 for PM, which a single-process job does not have).

*Deliberately not done.* (1) mTLS: the stub is not an SMO module and has no certificate (`mtls: "off"`), so with `SMO_MTLS=on` RAN NF OAM refuses its emit calls and the triggers answer 502; documented in `docker-compose.mtls.yml`, `conformance/README.md` and the stub's README; the alternative, giving the stub a certificate, would make a test double part of the trust domain. (2) PM-1 reads RAN NF OAM's answer, not DME records: a report is not stored, and reading DME back would need a data job and a token, which is a DME test rather than an adaptor test. (3) Nothing is emitted on a timer, and nothing retries: the stub is a trigger, not a simulator of a busy network. (4) No clear or acknowledge of alarms, VES, streaming or file-ready notifications: the kit covers the five routes RAN NF OAM takes from an adaptor. (5) A vendor adaptor needs a test hook of the trigger API's shape (or a shim) to be run through these groups; without `--oam-url` the kit runs the CM checks only. (6) The kit creates objects at RAN NF OAM and does not remove them, as in 9a.

### PR-SEC-4.7 — External Secrets example (built, not applied on a cluster)

- **Built.** `deploy/external-secrets/`: `secret-store-vault.yaml` (a `ClusterSecretStore`, Vault KV v2, Kubernetes auth), `external-secrets.yaml` (an `ExternalSecret` each for `smo-secrets` with `db-password` and `enrollment-secret`, `smo-role-secrets` with a `db-password-<role>` per `databaseRole` of `values.yaml`, the GUI OIDC client secret and one-time-code key, and the pattern for a module mTLS Secret `<module>-mtls`), `values.yaml` (the chart values that name them) and a README with the Vault Agent / Secrets Store CSI alternative in a short note. The ExternalSecrets are provider-neutral; only the store is Vault.
- **Kept in step with the chart.** `tests_integration/test_external_secrets_example.py` parses the example and checks the Secret names, the keys (including the role keys, derived from the modules in `values.yaml`), the `*SecretRef` defaults and the mTLS name and keys against `values.yaml` and the templates, that the example values set only keys the chart has, that every Secret created is read by the chart, that no Secret or secret value is in the files, and that the README says it has not been applied.
- **Not done, still open (SEC-4.7).** Applying it on a lab cluster with a real Vault and seeing the SMO start on the Secrets ESO creates: no cluster here. Not templated by the chart: Vault Agent or the CSI driver (the modules read files under `/run/secrets`, so it would work through an overlay, untried); no `PushSecret`.

### PR-RAPP-1 — signed rApp packages (RAPP-1.1 to 1.6)

Closed from `OPEN_ITEMS.md`. Before it, `signature_verified` was set true for every package that validated and nothing was checked against a key (`SECURITY.md` said so).

- **RAPP-1.1, the digest list.** `shared/smo_shared/csar_signing.py`. A signed CSAR carries `TOSCA-Metadata/DIGESTS.sha256`: one line `<sha-256 hex>  <path>` per file other than the two signing entries, sorted by path. `samples/build_csar.py` writes it through `signing_entries`; `scripts/csar_sign.py sign` adds it to an existing package (`sign_csar` rewrites the entries in the original order with fixed timestamps and replaces an earlier pair).
- **RAPP-1.2, the signature.** `TOSCA-Metadata/DIGESTS.sha256.sig`, JSON `{version: 1, algorithm: ed25519, keyId, signature}`: ed25519 over the exact bytes of the digest list. `keyId` is the SHA-256 of the 32 raw public-key bytes and only says which key to try. Library: `cryptography` (ed25519), already in the hashed lock as a dependency of `pyjwt[crypto]` (`mtls.py` and `gui-bff` use it too), so **no dependency was added and the lock was not recompiled** (`requirements/README.md` untouched). ed25519 is deterministic (RFC 8032), so a signed package built twice is byte-identical and the existing "committed `.csar` equals a rebuild" tests kept passing unchanged.
- **RAPP-1.3, the trust store.** `load_trust_store(path)`: a PEM file (one or several public keys) or a directory of `*.pub` / `*.pem`; dot-entries skipped and symlinks followed (a mounted ConfigMap); the publisher is the **file name** (`acme.pub` is `acme`, several keys in a file `acme`, `acme#2`). A store that cannot be used (missing, empty, not an ed25519 public key) is an error, never a smaller store. Configuration: `ONBOARDING_TRUST_STORE` (a path) and `ONBOARDING_REQUIRE_SIGNED_PACKAGES`, read for each package (a changed ConfigMap needs no restart), described in `docs/config_descriptions.json` and `docs/CONFIGURATION.md` (regenerated). **Not the `*_FILE` secret convention**: public keys are not secrets, so there is a path and no inline form (the same choice as `GUI_JWT_PREVIOUS_KEY_FILES`). Compose passes both (`${…:-}` and `${…:-false}`); the chart's `rappSigning.trustStoreConfigMap` mounts a ConfigMap read-only at `/run/rapp-trust` in the onboarding pod and `rappSigning.requireSigned` sets the flag, each rendered only when set, so the default pod template is unchanged (`tests_integration/test_helm_rapp.py`).
- **RAPP-1.4, verification in Onboarding.** `verify_signature` runs on the fetched bytes before anything is parsed. Refused, each with its own `SignatureError.code` and a message that names the file or the rule: a modified file, an added file, a removed file, a rewritten digest list, a signature made with another key under a trusted `keyId`, a key not in the store (unknown publisher), a digest list without a signature, a signature without a list, a malformed entry, an entry listed twice, a path with `..`, `/` or `\`. The message is `failureReason` of the `202` answer, prefixed `package signature: `. A package that carries signing entries is verified whenever a trust store is set, whatever the policy flag says: a tampered package is not "optional".
- **RAPP-1.5, the policy flag.** `ONBOARDING_REQUIRE_SIGNED_PACKAGES` (default `false`): an unsigned package is refused. With the flag and **no** trust store every package is refused with a message that says so (a misconfiguration must not read as "accept all"). With nothing set nothing changes, signed packages included. `signature_verified` keeps its old meaning (validated) while no trust store is configured, and is true only for a verified package once one is; an unsigned package that the policy lets through is false. The publisher is logged, not stored. This is the one place the "nothing changes" rule bends the truth: the field is not yet "verified against a key" for an operator who sets nothing. It is a decision for the owner when that default should flip (`docs/RAPP_PACKAGING.md` §8).
- **RAPP-1.6, the samples.** The four committed `.csar` are signed with a **demo publisher** key. Its private half is committed, and only under `samples/demo-signing/` (`demo-publisher.seed`, public key `demo-publisher.pub`, a README that says it must never be trusted in production; repeated in `samples/README.md`, `docs/RAPP_PACKAGING.md` §8.2, `docs/SECRETS.md`, the chart README and `values.yaml`). The private key is a one-line `ed25519-seed:` file, not a PEM block, so GitHub push protection and other secret scanners do not mistake it for a leak (`load_private_key` reads both). `tests_integration/test_rapp_signing.py` fails if any other private key (PEM or seed line) is committed anywhere in `smo/`, if a committed package differs from a rebuild, or if one no longer verifies against the demo key.
- **The tool.** `scripts/csar_sign.py` (`keygen`, `sign`, `verify`, `digests`; exit 0, 1 for a rejected package, 2 for a usage or file problem; the private key file is written 0600 and never overwritten) for a rApp developer and an operator; `verify` is the check Onboarding makes.
- **Tests.** `shared/tests/test_csar_signing.py` (38), `onboarding/tests/test_signing.py` (20, on the real route with real bytes and a real trust-store directory), `tests_integration/test_rapp_signing.py` (13), compose and chart parity in `tests_integration/test_helm_rapp.py`.
- **Moved the validation code.** The pure part of Onboarding's validation (`_asd_identity`, `_parse_ai_capabilities`, `_validate_runtime_profiles`, `_validate_limits`, `_parse_sme_declarations`, `PackageValidationFailed`) moved from `onboarding/app/main.py` to `app/package_validation.py` so that the offline validator (RAPP-3.1) runs exactly the code Onboarding runs; `main.py` re-exports the names (the unit tests and the fuzz target import them from there). Found on the way and fixed: a `TOSCA.meta` with no `Entry-Definitions:` line raised `StopIteration` from a bare `next()`, which nothing caught (a 500 instead of a `FAILED` package).
- **Not taken.** cosign (not available in the session that built this; keyless needs a transparency log and an identity provider an air-gapped operator lacks; a cosign signature can sit beside the ed25519 one later, the digest list is the same). X.509/CMS signatures as in the TOSCA CSAR specification: a certificate chain suits a public marketplace; the rest of this build trusts operator-held keys (invokers, enrolment). A certificate chain, expiry, revocation list and timestamp. Storing the publisher on the package row (a migration, and a GUI column). Checking the signature after onboarding. Making the trust store mandatory (the flag exists; the default is the owner's decision).

### PR-RAPP-2a — CPU and memory limits in the descriptor, and the rApp egress policy (RAPP-2.1, 2.3)

Closed from `OPEN_ITEMS.md`: RAPP-2.1 and RAPP-2.3. **RAPP-2.2 (the pod `securityContext`) stays open** in `OPEN_ITEMS.md`: it needs SB-16.2 and was not in this block.

- **RAPP-2.1, what the mapping was (confirmed in the code first).** AIMgF copied the manifest's `runtimeProfiles[mode]` (`{cpu, memory, gpu}`) as written into `workloadTemplate.resources` of the NFO descriptor it creates for a Training, Validation, Emulation or Inference runtime (`_resolve_runtime_profile`, `_nfo_create_execution_descriptor`, `_nfo_create_descriptor`); NFO stores the template and nothing reads `resources`. The descriptor Onboarding creates for the package itself carried only `toscaEntryDefinitions`: the manifest's profiles never reached it. `memory` was kept as an unchecked string.
- **RAPP-2.1, what it is now.** `shared/smo_shared/runtime_resources.py`: `container_resources(profile)` gives the `resources:` block of a Kubernetes container, `{requests: {cpu, memory}, limits: {cpu, memory}}`. AIMgF adds it as `workloadTemplate.containerResources` beside the unchanged `resources`; Onboarding adds `containerResourcesByMode` (one block per mode of `runtimeProfiles` that sets a CPU or memory) to the package's descriptor. A descriptor with nothing to set has neither key, so an unsized runtime and a package without profiles produce what they did. Decisions: **requests equal limits** (the profile states what the runtime needs; a pod that may use more than it asked for is the one a busy node squeezes, the `Guaranteed` class); fractional CPU in millicores; zero or absent sets nothing; **GPUs are not mapped** (the resource name is vendor specific, and where a GPU runs is NFO's decision through `requiredResourceTypeId`). `memory` must now be a Kubernetes quantity: Onboarding fails a package whose manifest has `memory: 16 GB` (new packages only; an onboarded package is untouched) and AIMgF answers 422 to an explicit `runtimeProfile` with one (the OpenAPI spec of AIMgF gained the `pattern`). "Descriptor shows them": `GET /nfo/descriptors` already returns `workloadTemplate`. Nothing applies them yet because there is no deployment manager behind NFO.
- **RAPP-2.3, the egress policy.** `deploy/helm/smo/templates/rapp-egress.yaml`, values `rappNetworkPolicy` (`enabled: false`). A NetworkPolicy, egress only, on the pods of the modules whose env says `SMO_IDENTITY_KIND: rapp` (the four sample rApps; selected by `app.kubernetes.io/name In [...]` and the release label, **not** by a new pod label, so no pod template changes and the rule that keeps the chart version out of pod templates holds; a render test compares the pod templates with the policy off and on). Allowed: R1 Termination's port; DNS (`dnsTo`, default kube-dns in `kube-system`); the bundled Postgres (`database: true`, because the reference rApps connect to the database as roles of their own; false for a workload that does not); the bundled Tempo's OTLP port when traces go there; `extraEgress`, verbatim. `extraPodSelectors` adds a policy per label selector for rApp workloads of an operator's own in the same namespace. Everything else, the Kubernetes API and the other modules included, is refused. Default off, so an upgrade changes nothing.
- **Verified how.** `helm` is not installed in the session that wrote it: the templates were read line by line and the new render tests (`tests_integration/test_helm_rapp.py`) are skipped without helm; CI's `helm` job runs them. The file-reading tests (defaults, compose parity, that the template selects by identity kind and adds no label) run everywhere. A NetworkPolicy also needs a CNI that enforces it; nothing here connects from a pod.
- **Not taken.** A pod label to select rApps by (it restarts them when the chart is upgraded). Mapping the GPU. A CPU limit above the request (burst) as a manifest option. Policies for ingress to the rApps. Policies in other namespaces than the release's. Pod `securityContext` (RAPP-2.2).

### PR-RAPP-3 — the rApp conformance pack (RAPP-3.1 to 3.3)

Closed from `OPEN_ITEMS.md`. `conformance/rapp/` (README, `kit.py`, `package_checks.py`, `runtime_checks.py`, `__main__.py`), modelled on `conformance/o1`: a registry of checks, `pass`/`fail`/`skip` plus `warn`, a report in JSON and Markdown (`--out`), exit 1 when a check fails, 2 on a usage problem.

- **RAPP-3.1, the offline validator.** `python -m conformance.rapp package FILE_OR_DIR [--trust PATH] [--require-signed]`. The verdict `PK-V` calls `onboarding/app/package_validation.py` (loaded by file path, because every module's package is called `app`): the same code `POST /packages` runs on a fetched package, so passing it means Onboarding accepts the package (the NFO call that follows is the one step not exercised). The other ten checks (structure, manifest, capabilities, SME, hygiene, signature) take its parsers apart so the developer sees which file is wrong, and add advice Onboarding does not give as warnings (ASD identity unset, no runtime profile for a declared mode, an unknown SDK namespace, a test suite or environment file shipped) and one extra failure (a private key inside the package). Passes on the four samples with their signatures verified; 13 crafted bad packages each fail the check that names the problem, and `tests_integration/test_rapp_conformance.py` compares PK-V with the real Onboarding app on the mesh for every one, so the two cannot drift apart.
- **RAPP-3.2, the runtime checks.** `python -m conformance.rapp runtime --package-url URL (--direct | --r1 URL --header …)`: RT-1 onboard (reads back `AVAILABLE` and a descriptor, shows `failureReason`), RT-2/3 register (instance `DEPLOYING` with identity and NFO workload, `bootstrap-complete` makes it `RUNNING`, one active usage registration), RT-4 heartbeat, RT-5/6 R1 usage (configuration round trip, the package's declarations, a warning fault that does not leave `RUNNING`), RT-7/8 terminate (`UNDEPLOYED`, workload and usage released, then deleted and 404). **Decisions:** the platform has no heartbeat route, so the heartbeat is the performance report a running rApp posts periodically, read back newest first; "R1 usage" is the calls a running rApp makes over R1, played by the kit with operator access (`--direct`, or a bearer token for `--r1`) and not with the instance's own OAuth credentials; the kit does not start the rApp's workload (the deployment does) and says so. Services are injected as clients, so the unit tests run it on the in-process mesh; a run cleans up after itself, also after a failure (instance terminated and deleted, package deleted so the same package can run again), and a step whose prerequisite failed is skipped with the reason.
- **RAPP-3.3, the report.** `--out report` writes `report.json` and `report.md`; the Markdown is also printed and, in CI, goes to the job summary.
- **Tests.** `tests_integration/test_rapp_conformance.py` (56 with its parametrisation): the samples pass; the crafted bad packages; advice is a warning; the signature cases; the CLI's exit codes and report files; the runtime pass twice in a row on each signed sample; eight fakes in front of one module, each breaking one thing, each caught by the check that reads it back; clean-up after a failure.
- **CI.** A step in the `compose-e2e` job ("rApp conformance pack, offline and against the stack"), after the O1 emitting-side step, in the same `python:3.11-slim` pattern: the offline command over the samples with the demo trust store and `--require-signed`, then a package server and the runtime command with `--direct` against the stack, each required to end "0 failed, 0 warnings" (runtime: "0 skipped" too), reports to the job summary. **Written without docker and not run: CI must confirm it** (the first run may need a fix of the step, not of the kit).
- **Not taken.** Running the rApp's workload, or driving it with its real OAuth credentials through the gateway (the kit would need the instance's secret and a token flow; `compose_e2e_roles.py` and `test_role_policy.py` cover roles). Autonomy enforcement, upgrade and rollback, the operator page's routes. A conformance badge or a registry of results. Checks of the egress policy from inside a pod.


### PR-AI-11, PR-AI-13 — a person approves an rApp's action; why an rApp acted (AI-11.1 to 11.5, AI-13.1 to 13.4, GUI-7.2); AI-12 not started

- **What existed first (checked, not assumed).** The AI-10 safeguards are all in RAN NF OAM's `POST /config-jobs`: kill switch (`_refuse_if_killed`), rate limit, blast radius and magnitude (`_enforce_rapp_limit`, `_enforce_change_limits`), each refusal recorded and announced through the outbox (`_refuse`); the gateway adds the role policy and its own kill-switch check. The autonomy modes are `rapp_instance.autonomy_mode` (`AUTONOMOUS`, `ASSIST`, `SHADOW`) and only the Intent Service's `AutonomyDispatch` acts on them (ASSIST waits at `AWAITING_SCOPE` until an operator resolves or rejects it, SHADOW is recorded and enacts nothing): a rApp that writes straight through DME `/actions` is not touched by its own mode. **There was no approval inbox in the GUI**: `PR-GUI-7` was all open, `PR-MGT-4` (change windows, `PENDING_APPROVAL` for a CM job) is not built, and the only "approval" in the code is AIMgF's model governance (`APPROVE`, `CERTIFY`, `decided_by`) and the ASSIST dispatch. So the task's "extend the existing inbox" became building the inbox for the one source that exists (GUI-7.2); 7.1 and 7.3 stay open and the page says they are not in it. The GUI had no Safeguards test either.
- **Where the hook is, and why there.** At RAN NF OAM, in `_execute_write`, after MSAC, the schema check and the dry-run branch and before the job row exists. That is the one place every config job passes whatever way it came (directly through the gateway, through DME on behalf of the rApp, through a replayed idempotent command), after every cheap refusal and before anything is written; a parked request is the request as the rApp sent it (`WriteConfigRequest.model_dump`), kept in a JSON column, and approving it runs the same function with the same body, so the approver approves what is dispatched, and the checks that depend on the network (the magnitude limit reads the current value) are run again at approval. Not the gateway (it cannot see the body of a config job, and holding there would hold the rApp's HTTP call), not the Intent Service's dispatch (it is not the only path to O1 and an ASSIST dispatch means "the operator picks the scope", not "the operator approves the write"), not a new `PENDING_APPROVAL` job state (it would need a CHECK-constraint change in a table that already needed one, `0026`, a job with no sub-changes and a state every consumer of `GET /config-jobs` must learn). Rollbacks, reverts and dry runs are not held: undoing a change must not wait for a person, and a dry run writes nothing.
- **What "approval mode" is.** A row in `rapp_approval_policy` keyed by the invoker id R1 vouches for (the same key as the limits and the kill switch, so a write made by DME for the rApp is held too). rApp Management sets it: `CreateInstance` takes `approvalPolicy {timeoutSeconds, onTimeout}`, accepted **only for an `ASSIST` instance** (422 `APPROVAL_POLICY_NEEDS_ASSIST` for the others: `AUTONOMOUS` means no one is asked, `SHADOW` writes nothing), stored on the instance (`approval_policy`, revision `0031`), pushed at bootstrap-complete beside the limits **and failing closed** (503, the instance stays `DEPLOYING`: an instance asked to wait for approval must not run writing at once because the push failed), removed on teardown, carried by an upgrade and restored by a rollback (`previous_approval_policy`). An admin can also set or remove it for any invoker from the GUI (Safeguards, **Approval…**) like the limits. An existing `ASSIST` instance is not held unless someone sets a policy: making `ASSIST` imply approval would have changed every running instance on upgrade.
- **The timeout (AI-11.3).** `timeoutSeconds` 60 to 604800 (default 3600), `onTimeout` `EXPIRE` (default, status `EXPIRED`) or `REJECT` (status `REJECTED`, `decided_by` `system:timeout`). Both write nothing; **there is no option that approves by itself**, and a schema test pins that `APPROVE` is a 422. The policy is copied onto the request when it is parked, so changing the policy later does not change what a waiting request becomes. Enforcement does not depend on a scheduler: the list, a read of one request and a decision lapse what is due themselves (a decision on a request that lapsed a second ago is a 409 that says so), `POST /rapp-approvals/expire-due` is the hook for an external scheduler, and the worker task `expire-approvals` (every minute) is the same code with a row lock that skips what another replica holds. Cost: a `GET` that writes.
- **Who may decide (AI-11.2).** Operators and admins (the GUI role table: a decision is as consequential as the CM write an operator may already make); setting which rApps wait, and the subscriptions, are admin. The decider is the signed-in user, pinned into `decidedBy` by the BFF (`smo-gui:<username>`), never the browser's value. An rApp can never decide, in three layers: the gateway's change allow-list does not contain the routes, `INTERNAL_ONLY` names them (and the two lists, which would name other rApps' actions), and RAN NF OAM refuses the `rapp` role (403 `ROLE_NOT_PERMITTED`) and a decider equal to the request's invoker id or `requestedBy` or a call carrying the requester's own id (403 `APPROVAL_SELF_DECISION`). An rApp reads its own request by id (`GET /rapp-approvals/{id}`, an unguessable UUID) and polls it; the SDK has `get_approval`.
- **Decided once, and a refused approval is closed.** The decision locks the row (`FOR UPDATE`; SQLite ignores it) and reads it past the identity map; a second decision is 409 `APPROVAL_NOT_PENDING`. If the checks refuse the request at approval (a kill switch thrown while it waited, a limit reached, MSAC or the schema now failing), the approver gets that refusal's own 403/422/429 and the request is closed `REFUSED` with `refusal_code`, so it does not sit in the inbox for a decision that cannot succeed. A safeguard refusal is also recorded in `safeguard_refusal` and announced as AI-10.6 does, because `_refuse` is the same function.
- **The notice (AI-11.5).** `approval_subscription` (callback URI, checked by the SSRF guard at subscribe time: 422, as for the safeguard subscribers), and `_notify_approvers`, one `enqueue` per subscriber **in the transaction that parks or lapses the request**: `RAPP_APPROVAL_REQUESTED` and `RAPP_APPROVAL_LAPSED`, with the rApp, requester, elements, change count and deadline but not the changes. One row in `docs/NOTIFICATIONS.md`. The inbox itself needs no subscriber (it polls every 5 s). Not built: a notice to the rApp itself that its request was decided (it polls), and a notice of a decision.
- **The decision record (AI-13.1, 13.2).** `decision {inputsRef, modelVersion, rationale, actionId}` is an optional field of `WriteConfigRequest` (DME's `ActionRequest.decision`, the SDK's `decision=`), validated and bounded (rationale 4000 characters). `rapp_decision_record` has one row per config job made **for an rApp** (`_acting_rapp`: the caller is an rApp, or an SMO module passes it on in `X-R1-On-Behalf-Of`; a module acting for itself, the GUI above all, is not an rApp's action) written in the job's transaction (so a failed job creation leaves no record and a record never exists without its job), plus one for an approval that ended without a job (`REJECTED`, `EXPIRED`, `REFUSED`; a request still waiting is not yet a decision). `disposition` says which: `DIRECT`, `APPROVED`, `ROLLBACK` (an rApp's undo), and so on. The record keeps references and text, not the changes (those are the job's sub-changes and the request) and not the inputs themselves.
- **Hash-chained through `smo_shared.audit`, without touching it (AI-13.1).** The record's `content_hash` is SHA-256 over its canonical JSON (every field but `audit_seq`), and `chain_decisions` writes one `audit.record(...)` row per record (`action` `DECISION`, target `/ran-nf-oam/decision-records/<id>`, `detail` with the id, the job and the hash) and stores the row's number in `audit_seq`. `GET /decision-records/{id}` recomputes the hash and compares it with the chain row: `VERIFIED`, `UNCHAINED` (not chained yet), `MISMATCH` (the record changed, or its audit row does not carry the hash: an attacker who edits a record and recomputes its own hash is still caught by the chain row, which the chain's own `verify` protects). It does not walk the chain; `python -m smo_shared.audit verify` does. **Chained after the commit, not in it**: the job's transaction can run for a minute (the dispatch retries), and the chain's head row is locked until the caller commits, so chaining inside it would stall the gateway's audit writes behind a southbound call. The cost is a window in which a record is `UNCHAINED`, closed in the request itself (best effort, logged) and by the worker's `chain-decisions` task; chaining picks one record at a time with `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` so two replicas never chain the same one. `smo_shared/audit.py` is untouched, so the mutation-tested module needed no new tests; RAN NF OAM's database role is granted `audit_log` and `audit_head` (`migrations/db_roles.json`).
- **The query (AI-13.3).** `GET /decision-records` filters by rApp, job, approval request, disposition, model version and time (`since` inclusive, `until` exclusive), newest first, with the `?total=false` convention; the list is internal-only at the gateway because it names other rApps' actions and their rationale. No filter on a managed element: the elements are a JSON list and a portable containment query would need a join table (not built).
- **The GUI (GUI-7.2, AI-13.4).** `/approvals`: a tab of what waits (polled every 5 s, with a count on the tab) and a tab of what was decided, a drawer per request (the changes, the rApp's rationale, model version and inputs reference, the job it became, and Approve / Reject with a reason for an operator; a viewer sees the request and no buttons) and a link to the decision record. `/decisions` and `/decisions/{id}`: filters, `Newer`/`Older` paging on `hasMore`, the outcome, the approver, and the integrity result with what each means. The config job drawer shows "Made for an rApp" with the rationale and a link when the job has a record. The Safeguards page shows each instance's approval policy and gives the admin **Approval…** and **Stop holding**. `useSmoPage` keeps the list envelope that `useSmo` unwraps. Vitest: the inbox, the decision list and record, the job drawer link, the policy dialog, the domain helpers; `scripts/gui_e2e.py` lists the two new pages for the browser check (not run here).
- **Not taken.** (1) A `PENDING_APPROVAL` job state (see above). (2) Holding at the gateway or in the Intent Service. (3) Auto-approval on timeout, or any option that writes without a person. (4) Two-person approval, approver groups, per-region or per-tenant approvers: that is the authorisation work that follows (revision `0032`, expand-only, built by another change). *Two-person approval was built afterwards, see "PR-AI-11 and PR-AI-13 follow-ups" below; approver groups and per-region approvers were not.* (5) Holding changes other than config jobs (DME data jobs, intents, training jobs). (6) Change-window approvals and model-gate approvals in the inbox (`GUI-7.1`, `7.3`). (7) Notifying the rApp or the approver of a decision. (8) The sample rApps sending a `decision` (the platform makes the record with empty fields; the four packages are unchanged, so no CSAR was rebuilt). *Built afterwards, see below.* (9) A purge of approval requests and decision records: both are kept (`docs/RETENTION.md`); deleting a decision record would be a tamper signal the chain cannot show for a removed row. *Built afterwards, off by default, and the argument above is answered below.* (10) A `SHADOW` flag per instance with a recording endpoint and a compare report (**AI-12 was not started**: it needs a decision on whether the shadow write is a policy row like the approval one, what "intended vs actual" compares against, and where the recording lives; the pieces it would reuse are the per-invoker policy table, the decision record with a `SHADOWED` disposition and the read-back of `GET /managed-entities/{me}/config`).
- **Verified here / only in CI.** Here: every module's unit suite touched (`ran-nf-oam` 628 tests, `rapp-mgmt`, `dme`, `sdk`, `r1-termination`, `gui-bff`), shared, Vitest, typecheck and build; the cross-service suite including the contract tests of every new route. Only CI: `scripts/check_migration_matches_models.py` and the migration round trip against Postgres for revision `0031` (its CHECK constraint, the explicit schema names, the new `audit_*` grants), the previous-release lanes, row locks between two replicas, the Helm render and the browser check.


### PR-SEC-10 — tenant and region authorization (SEC-10.1 to 10.6); SEC-10.7 and 10.8 open

- **What existed first (checked, not assumed).** The gateway knew who called (`X-R1-Invoker-Id`) and what kind of caller it was (`X-R1-Role`, `smo_shared/roles.py`), and every module trusted both. No module asked *which element* a call touched: a valid rApp token read and changed any managed element, alarm, job or file ("reads at R1 are open to every valid token" was written in `roles.py`, `docs/PRIVACY.md` and `docs/CONTROL_MATRIX.md`). The managed-element registry (`managed_entity`) had no place or owner column. `rapp_instance.region_scope` is something else (where an AUTONOMOUS instance's intents go), and the OAuth `scope` of a token is chosen by the caller in its token request, so neither could carry a restriction.
- **Where it is decided (SEC-10.1, `docs/adr/0005-tenant-region-authorization.md`).** In the module that owns the target, with one rule in `smo_shared/scope.py`; the gateway vouches for the caller's claim as it vouches for the id and the role. Enforcing at R1 Termination was weighed and refused: it sees a path and a body, so it would need every module's request shapes and a registry read per call, and it cannot filter a list, hide an item behind an id, re-check an approval weeks later or follow a call DME makes for an rApp. The ADR carries the semantic table, the failure modes and the status codes.
- **The rule (`smo_shared/scope.py`).** `Scope(regions, tenants)`; no claim is unscoped and is never asked anything; an axis the claim names must match exactly (case-sensitive, no wildcards or hierarchy); a target with no region/tenant, or not registered, is outside a claim that restricts that axis; a header that cannot be read is `DENY_ALL`. One rule in three forms: `permits(scope, region, tenant)`, `filter_statement` (a WHERE for lists) and `denied_condition` (the same refusals as a condition that is never NULL, for a subquery), proved equal row by row against SQLite for 7 claims over all 9 region/tenant combinations (`shared/tests/test_scope.py`). `covers(parent, child)` is the "never wider than yourself" check. The module is in the mutation pilot's `only_mutate` (and the gateway's `test_scope.py` is in its test selection).
- **The claim travels like the id (SEC-10.3).** SME stores it on the invoker (`invoker_registration.authz_scope`, JSON, revision `0032`), takes it at `POST /invoker-registrations` (`authzScope`, validated in the route with a fixed 422 `AUTHZ_SCOPE_INVALID` message so the input is not echoed) and from `PUT /invoker-registrations/{id}/authz-scope` (internal-only at the gateway: in `INTERNAL_ONLY` and not in `RAPP_MAY_CHANGE`; admin-only in the GUI backend), and returns it **read live** in the introspection as `authz_scope` (so an edit also limits the tokens already issued).
