R1 Termination keeps it in the cached answer (`introspection_cache.Caller(invoker_id, role, scope)`, was a 2-tuple), forwards it as `X-R1-Scope` (compact sorted JSON) and **drops both scope headers from every inbound request before it sets its own**; `X-R1-On-Behalf-Scope`, the claim of the rApp an SMO module acts for, is forwarded only from an `internal` caller, as `X-R1-On-Behalf-Of` is. A claim SME returns that is not valid becomes `DENY_ALL` at the gateway, never "no claim". `PUT .../authz-scope` through the gateway evicts that invoker's cache entries at once (the other replicas wait for the TTL, as for a revocation). The operator-API forward carries the claim too. Tests: `r1-termination/tests/test_scope.py` (18, including a spoofed header dropped for an unscoped caller and overwritten for a scoped one, and the operator-API path).
- **A module acting for an rApp (DME) passes the claim on.** `apply_correlation_id` installs `apply_scope_context`, which records the claim of the request's originator; `R1Client._headers` adds `X-R1-On-Behalf-Scope` beside `X-R1-On-Behalf-Of`. So a write the rApp makes through `POST /dme/actions` is held to its scope at RAN NF OAM, and the refusal is recorded against the rApp, not DME (`tests_integration/test_tenant_region_scope.py`, through the in-process mesh). Chosen over a lookup of the originator's claim at the gateway (a second introspection by id, and a second cache). The price: the claim of an on-behalf call is the module's word, as the id already is; a module that called another without `R1Client` would send none and be read as unscoped (every cross-module call goes through `R1Client`, the convention `CLAUDE.md` states).
- **A scoped caller cannot widen itself.** `POST /sme/invoker-registrations` is on the rApp change allow-list (the SDK registers), so a scoped rApp could have minted a fresh unscoped identity. SME now gives the new invoker the caller's claim, or a narrower one it names (`covers`), else 403 `SCOPE_DENIED`; a caller whose own claim is empty/`DENY_ALL` registers nothing. Found while writing the tests, not in the step list.
- **The target (SEC-10.2, revision `0032`).** `managed_entity.region` and `.tenant` (nullable, indexed), accepted by `POST /o1-adaptor-endpoints` (`region`, `tenant`, same alphabet and length as a claim value), changed by `PUT /managed-entities/{ref}/scope` (replaces both; an admin's call in the GUI backend), returned by `GET /managed-entities`, the endpoint list and the single read, and filterable (`?region=`, `?tenant=`). There is no discovery path that creates elements, so registration is the one place a place is set. The same revision adds the claim columns of SME and rApp Management and `rapp_action_approval.requester_scope`; the migration test, the downgrade and `check_migration_matches_models.py` (135 tables) pass on Postgres 16.
- **rApp Management (SEC-10.3).** `POST /instances` takes `authzScope`; the instance stores it (`rapp_instance.authz_scope`, `rapp_instance_version.previous_authz_scope`), puts it on the invoker it registers (at creation and on every new credential), keeps it through an upgrade and restores it on a rollback; `GET /instances` and `/instances/{id}` return it. **Fail closed:** if SME's answer does not echo the claim it was given (an SME of the previous release ignores the field), the invoker just made is deregistered and the create fails with 503; an unscoped identity is never kept where a scoped one was asked for. The claim is **not** a key of `manifest.yaml`: reach is granted by the operator who creates the instance, not claimed by the package's author.
- **The pilot (SEC-10.4).** `POST /config-jobs` checks the scope right after the kill switch and before the rate limit (a refused job does not use the budget), the change limits (which read the NF), MSAC and the schema (so a scoped caller learns nothing about an element it may not touch, not even a 422 on its attributes): any element outside refuses the whole request with **403 `SCOPE_DENIED`**, a dry run included. An unregistered element is refused exactly as an out-of-scope one (same status, title and detail shape), so the answer does not say which references exist. The refusal goes through the existing `_refuse`: recorded in `safeguard_refusal` (code `SCOPE_DENIED`, which `safeguard-subscriptions` and the GUI's refusal list now know) and announced. **Rollback** refuses unless every element the job wrote to is inside the scope (403, a detail that names none, dry run too); the automatic revert and the KPI-guard revert run on the job's own record, not as a caller's request, and are not scoped. **Approvals:** checked when the request is made; the requester's claim is kept with the parked request (`requester_scope`) and checked again at approval against the targets as they are then, so an element moved while the request waited closes it `REFUSED` / `SCOPE_DENIED` with nothing written. A snapshot, not a lookup of the rApp's current claim: RAN NF OAM does not hold claims and the approve path should not gain a synchronous dependency on SME; the consequence (narrowing a claim does not reach a parked request; reject it or stop the rApp) is in the ADR. The approver is an operator, unscoped.
- **Reads (SEC-10.5, 10.6).** By reference, 403: `GET /managed-entities/{ref}`, `.../config`, `.../config-history`, `.../config-history/diff`, `POST /pm-subscriptions`, `POST /fm-subscriptions`. By an id the system minted, 404 as if it did not exist: `GET /config-jobs/{id}` (a job with any element outside), `PATCH /alarms/{id}/ack|clear`, `GET /pm-files/{id}/file`. Filtered lists (never refused; `total` counts only what the caller may see): alarms, PM and FM subscriptions, `GET /files`, `GET /config-jobs` (only jobs all of whose elements are inside), managed entities, O1 endpoints, software jobs, `GET /cell-guards`. `GET /kpis/{name}` reads only the performance files of the elements inside the scope (`kpi.load_samples(scope=...)`), so a KPI over `all` is the caller's elements', not the network's. `DELETE` of a PM/FM subscription outside the scope answers 204 and removes nothing. Every lookup of an element's place is a column select, not an entity (the identity-map lesson of the earlier agents), proved by a test that moves an element and reads again in the same process.
- **The rApp's own calls (small, same PR).** `shared/smo_shared/roles.py` lacked `POST /rapp-mgmt/instances/{id}/bootstrap-complete` and `.../performance`, which the rApp Management README lists the rApp container as calling, so a container that followed the README was refused (403). Added to `RAPP_MAY_CHANGE`; rApp Management applies the existing `_own_instance_or_operator` (an rApp's invoker id must be this instance's `oauthClientId`, else 403 `NOT_THIS_INSTANCE`) to both. The route-table walks (`test_role_policy.py`) check the new entries match real routes and that an rApp is refused everywhere else; `r1-termination/tests/test_roles.py` pins the near misses (other methods, `.../fault`, `.../recover`, `.../credentials`, `.../upgrade`, `PUT .../config`, extra or missing segments). **A second finding, from tracing what the call does next:** when an rApp itself calls `bootstrap-complete`, rApp Management puts the instance's limits and approval policy in force at RAN NF OAM through `R1Client`, which adds `X-R1-On-Behalf-Of: <the rApp>` because the request being handled came from that rApp; RAN NF OAM's `_not_own_limit` then reads the caller as the rApp and refuses it (`RAPP_LIMIT_SELF_CHANGE`), which fails the bootstrap closed (503). Nothing had shown it because only an operator had ever called `bootstrap-complete`. Fixed with `smo_shared.invoker.on_own_account()`, a block in which the module acts for nobody, around `_on_bootstrap` (the limits are the platform's act about the rApp, not the rApp's); tests in `shared/tests/test_on_behalf_of.py` and `rapp-mgmt/tests/test_scope.py` (which fails without it). (The Go SDK's author had recorded the same gap as a documented exception in `sdk-go/README.md` and `tests_integration/test_sdk_go_example.py`; the exception is deleted there and the note says it is closed.) The same finding applies to `POST .../fault` and `PUT .../config`, which the README also lists for the rApp container and which are still refused: not added (the SDK and the samples call neither), the README now says so.
- **GUI.** The Infrastructure page's O1 endpoint table has a Region / tenant column and the register form takes both; the rApp page shows the instance's access scope ("regions eu-west · tenants acme", or "Unscoped (every managed element)"); `SCOPE_DENIED` is in the refusal list and meanings of the Safeguards page; the GUI backend allows the two new `PUT` routes to an admin only (`rbac.py`, the permission fixture regenerated). There is no form to edit a claim or an element's place yet (an admin does it through the API).
- **Not taken.** (1) Enforcement at the gateway (above). (2) Wildcards, region hierarchy, cell-level scope. (3) A scope in the manifest, or on the OAuth token request. (4) A lookup of the originator's current claim for an on-behalf call, or for an approval. (5) Scoping the operator (GUI users) and the approvers: that is the session claim of `GUI-5.1`. (6) An ownership rule for jobs (an rApp rolling back another rApp's job inside its scope). (7) Row-level security in Postgres and the OPA sidecar (`SEC-10.8`). (8) Recording a refused *read* as a safeguard refusal.
- **Still open.** `SEC-10.7`: DME and MLMR carry no managed element, region or tenant, so there is nothing for the rule to match; the one DME route that names an element (`POST /dme/actions`) is scoped at RAN NF OAM, `GET /dme/actions` was not filtered. In RAN NF OAM, not yet scoped: the managed-object tree and topology, the KPI schedules, the file subscriptions, the vendor and CM-schema registries. (Both closed afterwards: "PR-SEC-10 (follow-up)" below.) `OPEN_ITEMS.md`, PR-SEC-10.
- **Verified here / only in CI.** Here: `shared` (including `test_scope.py`), `r1-termination` (284), `sme`, `ran-nf-oam` (673), `rapp-mgmt`, `gui-bff`, the cross-service suite with Postgres 16 (migration round trip, `check_migration_matches_models.py`), the contract tests of every route changed, Vitest, typecheck and build, `mutation_pilot.sh`, `ruff` and `mypy` for the modules touched. Only CI: Postgres 18, the previous-release lanes (`check_previous_release_code.sh`, `check_upgrade_from_previous_release.sh`) for revision `0032`, the Helm render, the browser check, and a stack with a real gateway in front of a scoped rApp (the gateway and the modules are tested together only through the in-process mesh).

### PR-RAPP-4 (Go) — the Go SDK and its example rApp (RAPP-4.1; RAPP-4.2 to 4.5 for Go)

RAPP-4.1 was decided by the owner: both a Java and a Go SDK. This entry is the Go half (`sdk-go/`, `sdk-go/README.md` has the HLD, LLD and tests); the Java SDK is a separate change and keeps RAPP-4.2 to 4.5 open in `OPEN_ITEMS.md` until it lands.

- **RAPP-4.2, models and clients: hand-written over `net/http`, not generated.** The inputs are FastAPI's OpenAPI 3.1 documents: optional fields are `anyOf [T, null]`, every route repeats one `ErrorEnvelope` for its errors, and R1 Termination's document is one catch-all `/{full_path}` proxy route, so a generator would emit hundreds of types for 20 modules of which an rApp uses a dozen routes; and `oapi-codegen` (a pinned tool plus its runtime module in `go.sum`, and 3.1 support that was not complete) or `openapi-generator` (a JVM) would put a dependency or a build step that can drift where the standard library does the job. So: standard library only, `go.mod` with no `require` and therefore no `go.sum`; `tests_integration/test_sdk_go_example.py` fails if either appears. What generation would have given, a check that the client follows the documents, is `routes.go` (every route the SDK calls: document, gateway prefix, method, template) and `routes_test.go`, which looks each up in `docs/openapi/<module>.json`; request and response bodies are `Object`s or small literals and are not checked there. Typed helpers cover only what the example and a typical start-up use (`Data`: types, producers, producer status; `Models`: list; `Platform`: provider registration, discovery; `RApp`: instance, operator API get, put and delete, bootstrap-complete, performance); everything else is `Client.Do`. Not taken: generating the six namespaces' types (revisit if the SDK grows to cover them), a `Page` that drops the counts like the Python `ensure_ok` (Go keeps `total`, `limit`, `offset`, `hasMore`), and typed result structs (the SDK does not re-model the platform's schemas).
- **RAPP-4.3, token acquisition and refresh.** `auth.go` is `smo_shared.r1_client._ModuleIdentity` for an rApp: `GET /bootstrap` (with `X-Bootstrap-Key`), `POST /invoker-registrations` without the enrollment header (SME records an `rapp`), `POST /oauth2/token` as `client_credentials` for scope `smo-rapp`; the token is cached until `expires_in` minus 30 s and renewed under a mutex, and once on a 401 (the rejected token is compared, so concurrent 401s renew once). Two deliberate differences from Python: a failed token flow is an error and the call is not sent unauthenticated; and a pinned invoker (`SMO_INVOKER_ID`/`SECRET`, the identity rApp Management issued, which `PUT .../operator-api` checks) is never replaced after `invalid_client`, only an invoker the process enrolled itself is (Python re-enrolls on any 400, which would silently replace the instance's identity). An enrolled identity is not deregistered on exit: an rApp may not delete invoker registrations under `roles.py`. Retry (`retry.go`): 4 attempts, 200 ms doubling to 5 s, equal jitter, `Retry-After` up to 30 s, context-aware; transport errors, 502 and 504 are repeated only for calls safe to repeat (GET, PUT, DELETE, POST with the `Idempotency-Key` every POST carries, reused by the repeat), the enrollment POST only on 429/503; a `409 CONCURRENT_MODIFICATION` write is repeated once. Errors (`errors.go`): one `*Error` with status, title, detail and the first 4 KiB, from the four answer shapes of the platform. The "test against the stack" half is **not done**: the 57 unit tests (92 % of the library) use an `httptest` fake built from the real handlers' shapes, and the example binary was run against a scripted stand-in only.
- **RAPP-4.4, the example (`examples/hello-rapp`).** A rApp in Go: token, `RegisterOperatorAPI` for its instance (`HELLO_INSTANCE_ID`), a heartbeat that reads `GET /dme/dme-types`, an operator API on `:8000` serving the two routes of the `operatorUi` page its manifest declares (status, "Heartbeat now"), and on SIGTERM `ClearOperatorAPI` and exit 0. The package is `package/` (manifest, capabilities, ASD, `TOSCA.meta`) built by `build_csar.py` through `samples/build_csar.py`'s own `build_bytes` (the CSAR is not committed); the container is `CGO_ENABLED=0` in a `FROM scratch` image (non-root, builder pinned by digest, a `-probe` healthcheck since there is no shell) with a compose override file; `docker compose config` passes with it, the image itself was **not built** (no Docker daemon where it was written). Finding recorded in `sdk-go/README.md` 2.5: `bootstrap-complete` and `performance` are not on `roles.py`'s rApp allow-list (only `operator-api` is), though `rapp-mgmt/README.md` lists the rApp container as their caller and the runbook calls them directly at `rapp-mgmt:8000`; the SDK has both methods, the example does not call them, and a test pins the two as the refused ones. "Terminate" is the rApp leaving cleanly; terminating the instance is an operator's call to rApp Management.
- **RAPP-4.5, CI.** Job `sdk-go` in `smo-tests.yml` (no path filter: the workflow's jobs are required checks): `actions/setup-go` pinned by SHA, version from `go.mod`, no cache (nothing to cache), `gofmt`, `go vet`, `go test -race -count=1 -cover`, `go build` and the example's static binary. Dependabot watches `gomod` for `sdk-go` and the example Dockerfile's digest. Not seen green when written: the Go toolchain here is 1.24.7 and everything passed locally, the first run on GitHub is the check.
- **Tests.** `sdk-go` 60 (57 in the library: token flow 15, retry and `Do` 24, errors 3, namespaces 7, config 6, routes 2, with subtests 66; 3 in the example) and `tests_integration/test_sdk_go_example.py` (8). Two deliberate mutations (margin to 0, 401 renewal off) fail the tests meant to catch them.

### PR-RAPP-4 (Java) — the Java SDK and its example rApp (RAPP-4.1 decided; RAPP-4.2 to 4.5 done for Java)

- **RAPP-4.1, the decision.** Both Java and Go; the Go half is `PR-RAPP-4 (Go)` above.
- **RAPP-4.2, clients: written by hand over `java.net.http`, not generated.** The deciding fact: in `docs/openapi/*.json` almost every response schema is empty (`{}`; for example `GET /rapp-mgmt/instances/{id}/performance`), and only some request bodies are modelled (`CreateInstanceRequest`, `DataJobRequest`, `OperatorApiRequest`). A generator would produce thousands of lines of classes that carry no response types, a Java client that is 25 modules wide when a rApp uses four, and a toolchain (openapi-generator's jar plus its own dependency tree and a template choice) to pin. Hand-written thin clients return Jackson `JsonNode`, as the Python SDK returns `dict`. What keeps them honest is `RoutesContractTest`: each route in `Routes.ALL` (method, path template, query parameters, top-level body fields) is compared with the committed `docs/openapi/<module>.json`; a route renamed or removed, a query parameter not declared, or a required body property not sent fails the build. It runs offline (the JSON files are in the repository), needs no service, and is the Java counterpart of the platform's own spec-equals-schema check. Rejected: openapi-generator (`java` native library) and Gradle (a wrapper downloads its distribution too, no gain over Maven's). Runtime dependency: `jackson-databind` 2.22.3 only; test: JUnit Jupiter 5.11.4.
- **RAPP-4.3, tokens.** `TokenProvider` mirrors `smo_shared/r1_client.py`: `GET /bootstrap` (with `X-Bootstrap-Key` when set) for the token endpoint, `POST /invoker-registrations` (opaque label `smo-rapp:<MODULE>:<random>`, `X-SMO-Enrollment` only for `SMO_IDENTITY_KIND=module`), `client_credentials` grant with scope `smo-rapp` (`smo-internal` for a module), expiry margin 30 s, one refresh on a 401, one fresh enrolment when SME answers `invalid_client`. Same variables: `R1_GATEWAY_URL`, `SMO_INVOKER_ID`/`SMO_INVOKER_SECRET`, `SMO_BOOTSTRAP_KEY[_FILE]`, `SMO_IDENTITY_KIND`, `SMO_ENROLLMENT_SECRET[_FILE]`, `MODULE`. Differences, chosen on purpose: no token is an `SdkException` (Python logs and lets the gateway answer 401); a pinned invoker that SME no longer knows is an error, never replaced by a fresh enrolment (it would not be the identity rApp Management issued for the instance); `close()` deregisters an invoker the process enrolled itself. `R1Client` adds the Python SDK's `_RetryOnConflict` (one repeat on `409 CONCURRENT_MODIFICATION`, same `Idempotency-Key`) and a retry policy Python does not have (4 attempts, 200 ms doubling to 5 s with jitter, `Retry-After` honoured up to 30 s, on 429/502/503/504 and transport errors). Not done: `SMO_MTLS` from the environment (pass an `HttpClient` with an `SSLContext`).
- **RAPP-4.4, the example.** `examples/hello-rapp`: enrols, `PUT`s its operator API base at rApp Management, reports a heartbeat through `POST /rapp-mgmt/instances/{id}/performance` (the platform has no heartbeat route for a rApp; the newest performance report is what the GUI shows), on "Run now" reads DME types and MLMR models, serves `/live`, `/ready` and the three operator routes of its declared `operatorUi`, and on SIGTERM sends a last heartbeat, clears the operator API and deregisters. `samples/build_csar.py` gained `--source-dir/--name` (the sample builds are unchanged and still byte-identical) so the package is built the same way as the samples. The Dockerfile is multi-stage with digest-pinned images (the `maven:3.9.11-eclipse-temurin-21-noble` image builds, Temurin 21 JRE runs; the first CI image build showed the Maven wrapper failing its distribution SHA-256 check inside the build, so the image no longer uses the wrapper: the Maven image is the pin there); compose is an override file because the stack's service list (and the Helm chart that must match it) is not changed by an example. `sdk/tests/test_java_example_package.py` checks the package with Onboarding's code. **Not verified:** a run against the live stack (no Docker daemon was available when this was written); the example is covered by a stand-in platform in its unit tests.
- **RAPP-4.5, CI.** Job `sdk-java` in `smo-tests.yml` (that workflow has no path filter, because its jobs are required checks): `actions/setup-java` pinned by SHA (Temurin 21), `./mvnw -C verify`, `scripts/verify-dependencies.sh` and a `docker build` of the example. Supply chain: every version fixed in the POM, `requirePluginVersions` enforced, the Maven distribution pinned by SHA-256 in `.mvn/wrapper`, and `dependencies.sha256` holds the SHA-256 of each of the 11 jars of the test classpath (Maven has no lock file); `.github/dependabot.yml` gained the `maven` and `docker` entries for `sdk-java`.
- **RAPP-4 Java SDK: the Dependabot group bump (JUnit 6, Maven plugins, Maven 3.10.0) and why its first CI run failed.** Dependabot's grouped PR moved JUnit Jupiter 5.11.4 to 6.1.3, ten Maven plugins (compiler 3.16.0, surefire 3.6.0, jar 3.5.1, resources 3.5.0, install 3.2.0, clean 3.5.0, dependency 3.11.0, enforcer 3.6.3, site 3.22.0, deploy 3.2.0) and the Maven wrapper (3.3.2 to 3.3.4, distribution 3.9.11 to 3.10.0). It cannot regenerate `dependencies.sha256`, so the step *The resolved jars are the pinned ones* failed, as designed: the pin did its job. Done by hand with the repository's own mechanism: `scripts/verify-dependencies.sh --write`, the diff reviewed (the six JUnit and platform jars changed version; JUnit 6 adds one dependency, `jspecify-1.0.0.jar`, so the pin is 12 jars, not 11; the jackson, apiguardian and opentest4j hashes are unchanged). The wrapper's `distributionSha256Sum` was checked against the zip downloaded from Maven Central (SHA-256 `1f6d99...bddaff`; the SHA-512 Apache publishes beside it also matches), not taken from the PR on trust. No test code needed to change: JUnit 6 needs Java 17, the build is on 21, and the tests use only `org.junit.jupiter.api`. The example's Dockerfile stays on `maven:3.9.11-eclipse-temurin-21-noble` (a digest pin Dependabot's docker ecosystem moves on its own); the image's `package` command was run with a Maven 3.9.11 distribution against the new POM and passes. **Only CI shows** the `docker build` of that image itself and a resolution on a clean runner cache.

### PR-MGT-14, PR-MGT-15 — zero-touch onboarding and software campaigns (MGT-14.1 to 14.5, MGT-15.1 to 15.4); the GUI, notifications and a real software exchange open

Release 0.7.0 scope row 5 (stretch). One module owns both (RAN NF OAM: managed elements, config jobs, software jobs), so the code is `ran-nf-oam/app/lifecycle.py` (a router, like `vendors.py`), three tables and four nullable columns in revision `0033`, and the existing routes with a few lines each. The rule for both: **opt in, nothing changes for an existing user until they act.**

- **Onboarding is a record per element, and the template is just a config job (14.1 to 14.5).** A template (`onboarding_template`, key `name`) is `entityType`, optionally `vendorName`, a list of changes without an element (the new element is the target), a baseline version and two switches. Registration (`POST /o1-adaptor-endpoints`, "discovery" in this build: there is no registry to poll) calls `lifecycle.on_registered` in the registration's own transaction: it picks the enabled template for the entity type, a vendor-specific one before a general one, then by name, and writes `element_onboarding` (`DISCOVERED` -> `TEMPLATE_SELECTED`, or `NO_TEMPLATE`). **If no enabled template exists it returns before creating anything**, so registration is byte-for-byte what it was: no row, no new key in the answer (the answer has an `onboarding` object only when a row was made), no extra query beyond one existence check. Applying (`apply_template`) builds a `WriteConfigRequest` from the template and calls `_execute_write`, so MSAC, the schema check, dispatch, snapshots and the rollback of 1.6 all apply, and the config job is visible in `GET /config-jobs` like any other (`requestedBy` `onboarding:<template>`). `APPLYING` is committed before the write, so two replicas (or a double click) cannot both write: the second gets 409 from the FSM; and a failure after that leaves a row that says `FAILED` and why, with a major alarm `onboarding:<ref>`, never a row stuck in `APPLYING` (an unexpected exception also marks it `FAILED` and is then re-raised). `autoApply` fires on the first heartbeat (`DISCOVERED` -> `ACTIVE`), after the heartbeat's own commit, and cannot fail the heartbeat.
- **The baseline check (14.4).** The element's software version is not known to this build anywhere else, so registration takes an optional `softwareVersion` (and apply takes one to update it) and the row keeps it. `MATCH`, `MISMATCH` or `NOT_CHECKED`; a mismatch is flagged on the row and as one warning alarm (not one per check). `requireBaseline` turns the flag into a stop: a mismatch, or a version never reported, fails the apply before any job is made. Not taken: reading the version from the element over O1 at apply time (the software inventory is not modelled; it would be the first thing the SWM exchange of `MGT-15.8` provides).
- **Not retroactive, on purpose.** Defining a template does not touch elements registered earlier: they have no row, and `GET /element-onboarding/{ref}` says so (404) until an operator calls `POST /element-onboarding/{ref}/select`, which makes the row (that is also the way to select again after a template changed). The alternative, matching every existing element when a template appears, would turn the first `PUT /onboarding-templates` into a fleet-wide state change.
- **A campaign is waves of the existing software jobs (15.1, 15.2).** `software_campaign` holds the ordered element list (fixed at creation, named or selected by entity type, vendor, region, tenant; only elements with the SWM service and, for a scoped caller, only elements in its scope), the wave settings and an event log. A wave starts one software job per element together (`lifecycle.start_software_job`, the same function `POST /software-management-jobs` now uses), tagged `campaign_id` and `campaign_wave`. An element that already has a software job running refuses the whole campaign (409): two campaigns, or a campaign and a hand-started job, cannot fight over an element. The campaign has no runner of its own: a job of a campaign calls `on_job_advanced` after its own commit (in a second transaction, so the job's advance cannot be lost to a conflict on the campaign; if another request moved the campaign first it catches `StaleDataError` and the sweep catches up), and `advance_due` (run by the worker's `advance-waves` task, so no new task or Helm change) continues elapsed pauses and catches up running campaigns. The same `HALTED` idea as `MGT-5` (the state between waves, with a reason), the same `WAVE_PAUSE_NOT_ELAPSED`, and the gate list `HEALTH_GATES` with the same shape: a failed job, then new critical or major alarms since the wave started above `gateMaxNewAlarms`. **Unlike MGT-5 the gate also runs after the last wave** (a failed job in the last wave must not read as `COMPLETED`); continuing past a failed gate at the end is the operator's decision and the report shows the failed jobs.
- **Rollback (15.3) is one revert software job per completed job.** A revert job carries `rollback_of`; the campaign is `ROLLING_BACK` until they all end, then `ROLLED_BACK` or `ROLLBACK_FAILED` (rolling back again retries only the ones whose revert failed). Automatic with `onGateFailure: rollback`, by hand from `HALTED`, `COMPLETED`, `ABORTED` or `ROLLBACK_FAILED`; refused (422) while a job of the campaign still runs, because that job would complete unreverted. The state of each original job is not changed (it stays `COMPLETED`); the report shows `revert`. **Honest limit:** the software job is still the Phase 1 stub (phases reported by `advance`, no southbound exchange, no version on the wire), so a revert job is a job that names the job it undoes, not a request to install the previous version. The campaign orders, gates and records; it does not tell an element what to install (`MGT-15.8`).
- **The report (15.4)** is computed from the jobs and the event log, not stored: per wave and element the job, phase, status and revert; totals; and `attention` (a failed job, a failed revert, elements never reached when the campaign was aborted, rolled back or completed over a failed gate).
- **Schema (`0033`, expand only).** Three new tables (CHECK constraints on the status columns, added to the CHECK-vs-enum test) and four nullable columns on `software_management_job` plus an index on `campaign_id`; all rows get NULL, the previous release's code does not read any of it, the downgrade drops it. `element_onboarding` has a foreign key to `managed_entity` (as `software_management_job` does); `template_name` is deliberately not a key. `migrations/table_owners.json` lists the tables.
- **Scope and roles.** Reads and actions are scoped by element (SEC-10): a campaign with an element outside the caller's scope is a 404, the list leaves it out, creating one with an outside element is 403, a selector only selects what the caller may touch; onboarding is by element reference, so 403. The gateway's allow-list for an rApp's changes already refuses every new `POST`/`PUT`/`DELETE`; the GUI backend's table lists them (templates admin; select, apply and the campaign actions operator, `requestedBy` pinned to the signed-in user; `advance-due` is not exposed).
- **Error codes** `ONBOARDING_TEMPLATE_NOT_FOUND`, `ELEMENT_ONBOARDING_NOT_FOUND`, `SOFTWARE_CAMPAIGN_NOT_FOUND` live in `lifecycle.py`, not in `smo_shared.errors`: that module is under the mutation pilot, and a change to it would have meant a mutation run for three constants. Moving them is a three-line change if the owner prefers the shared list.
- **Not taken.** (1) Notifications on a failed onboarding or a halted campaign (an alarm and the state are the signals; adding an event means an entry in `docs/NOTIFICATIONS.md` and a subscription API: `MGT-14.7`, `MGT-15.6`). (2) GUI pages (`MGT-14.6`, `MGT-15.5`): the backend allows the calls so a page can be added without a backend change. (3) Matching existing elements when a template appears (above). (4) Template placeholders beyond the element (`MGT-14.8`). (5) A job timeout and a reverse-wave rollback (`MGT-15.7`). (6) A KPI gate (`MGT-15.9`, after `MGT-11`). (7) Choosing campaign elements by topology (the selector is region, tenant, vendor and entity type).
- **Verified here / only in CI.** Here: `ruff`, `mypy` (`scripts/run_mypy.sh ran-nf-oam`), the `ran-nf-oam` suite (743, of which 69 are new; coverage 97.9 against a floor of 97) and `gui-bff`, Postgres 16: the migration round trip (up, down one, up), `check_migration_matches_models.py`, the regenerated OpenAPI specs and the cross-service suite. Only CI: the two upgrade lanes (the previous release's code on the new schema, an upgrade that keeps its data), the contract test against a running stack, the Helm render, Vitest (no `node_modules` here; only the generated permissions fixture changed in the GUI).

### PR-MGT-14.6 — GUI pages, notifications, a job timeout and a reverse-order rollback for onboarding and campaigns (MGT-14.6, 14.7, MGT-15.5, 15.6, 15.7); the real software exchange and the KPI gate stay open

The rows of `PR-MGT-14`, `PR-MGT-15` above that needed no host of ours. Same rule: **opt in, nothing changes for an existing user.** Revision `0035` (expand only) is the one schema change.

- **GUI (14.6, 15.5).** Two tabs of Infrastructure, not new routes (so `scripts/gui_e2e.py`'s page list, which the router test keeps equal to the router, is unchanged): **Onboarding** (`pages/Onboarding.tsx`) and **Software campaigns** (`pages/Campaigns.tsx`). Onboarding: the template table with a modal editor (admin; a viewer or operator opens the same row as a read-only view), the per-element table with its state, template, software check, the detail of a failure and the config job (drawer), **Apply** (modal with the software version the element runs) and **Select…** (best match or a named template; also for an element that has no row yet), each offered only where the state machine accepts it (`lib/lifecycle.ts` `onboardingActions`/`campaignActions`, unit-tested against a table written from the two state machines, so no button is meant to end in a 409). Campaigns: the list with a state filter, **Start a campaign…** (named elements or a selector, wave size, pause, alarms allowed, job timeout, gate action, rollback order, and **Preview waves**, which is the API's `dryRun`), the drawer from `GET …/report` (settings, totals, attention, each wave's jobs with phase, state, revert and `timed out`, the event log) with **Continue** (with a "do not wait for the pause" box when it is the pause that holds), **Halt**, **Abort**, **Roll back** (a confirmation each where it destroys something). The pages never send `requestedBy`: the BFF's rules already pin it to the signed-in user, and reads are a viewer's, the writes an operator's, the templates and subscriptions an admin's, as `0033`'s rules had them. One addition there: `POST`/`DELETE /ran-nf-oam/lifecycle-subscriptions` are admin rules (`rbac.py`, the permissions fixture regenerated with `gui-bff/scripts/export_permissions.py`). The GUI starts a campaign with `rollbackOrder` **reverse**; the API's own default is still `all` (below). The shared `Modal` and `Drawer` got an accessible name (`aria-labelledby` on the heading): axe-core found a dialog without one the first time it was run on a dialog, and no earlier page had run it on one.
- **Notifications (14.7, 15.6).** A new subscription resource, `/lifecycle-subscriptions` (`POST {callbackUri, events?}`, `GET`, `DELETE /{id}`; table `lifecycle_subscription`), shaped like the safeguard and approval subscriptions and, like them, internal-only at R1 (`INTERNAL_ONLY` in `smo_shared/roles.py`, tested in the gateway's `test_roles.py`) and an admin's in the GUI. A destination the SSRF guard refuses is a 422 at subscription time. Three events: `ONBOARDING_FAILED` (in `_fail`, every end of an apply in FAILED: a refused write, a rejected sub-change, an unexpected error, a required baseline that did not match), `CAMPAIGN_HALTED` (`_halt`, for `GATE_FAILED` and `OPERATOR_HALT`, including an operator turning the pause into a hold; **not** for `WAVE_PAUSE`: every campaign with a pause would announce itself between every two waves) and `CAMPAIGN_ROLLBACK_FAILED` (`_progress_rollback`). One call site (`lifecycle.py::_notify_lifecycle`, one row in `docs/NOTIFICATIONS.md`, class A, in the outbox from the start): an outbox row per subscriber that wants the event, in the transaction that records the failure, so the notice exists exactly when the failure does and a rollback of the transaction sends nothing; no subscriber, one empty query and no row. The text is the platform's own: a reason code, a count, the row's `detail`; an unexpected error shows only the exception's class name, as the row always did (a test checks `disk on fire` is nowhere in the notice). Only a caller-supplied URL travels, and only through the outbox (`smo_shared.webhook`). **Not taken:** a notice for a software baseline that does not match but does not stop the onboarding (a warning alarm and the row are the signal; `MGT-14.7a`), and one for a gate failure under `onGateFailure: rollback`, where the campaign is never HALTED and only a rollback that fails is announced (`MGT-15.6a`).
- **A job that never reports (15.7).** `jobTimeoutSeconds` on `POST /software-campaigns` (1 to 604800, absent: no timeout, which is every campaign made before and the default). The clock is `software_campaign.wave_started_at`, which already existed for the alarm gate and is set again when a reverse-rollback step starts; the sweep (`advance_due`, the worker's `advance-waves`, so no new task and no Helm change) calls `expire_jobs` before `progress`, and fails every job of the running wave (or the revert jobs of a rollback) that is still `IN_PROGRESS`: the same `PHASE_FAILED` event, the phase frozen, a `JOB_TIMED_OUT` entry in the campaign's log naming the job. The campaign then decides as for any failed job (the gate halts it or rolls it back; a revert that timed out ends the rollback `ROLLBACK_FAILED`), and the report marks the job `timedOut` and words its `attention` line accordingly. A job's own clock, from its last report, would have needed a timestamp on the software job table; the wave's clock needed none. The consequences, stated: the time counts from the wave's start, not from the last report; a halt by an operator does not stop it; the resolution is the sweep's 15 s and it needs the worker. A late report for a job that has been failed is refused: `advance` now answers 409 for any job whose state does not allow the report, where it was an unhandled 500 (the one change to an existing route's behaviour, and only for a call that failed before).
- **Reverse-order rollback (15.7).** `rollbackOrder` (`all` | `reverse`; column `rollback_order NOT NULL DEFAULT 'all'` with a CHECK). `all` is what `0033` did. `reverse` starts revert jobs for the highest wave that still has a completed, not-undone job, and starts the next lower wave only when every revert job started so far has ended and none failed (`_progress_rollback`, run by the same job-advance hook and sweep as before); a failed revert ends the rollback ROLLBACK_FAILED with the earlier waves untouched, and rolling back again picks up where it stopped (the failed ones and every wave not yet reached). `ROLLBACK_WAVE_STARTED` in the log per step. **Decision, not taken:** `reverse` as the API default. It would change what a rollback does for a client that sends no `rollbackOrder`; with `NOT NULL DEFAULT 'all'` every existing campaign, and every campaign the previous release's code creates, keeps today's behaviour. Making `reverse` the default for new campaigns is a one-word change in the request model plus a release note, left to the owner (`MGT-15.6a`). The GUI's start form already defaults to it.
- **Schema (`0035`, expand only).** One new table (`lifecycle_subscription`, in `table_owners.json` under `ran-nf-oam`), one nullable column and one column with a default on `software_campaign` (`job_timeout_seconds`, `rollback_order` with `software_campaign_rollback_order_check`). The previous release's code never reads the table or the nullable column and its INSERT of a campaign does not name `rollback_order`, so it gets `'all'`, which is what it does. The CHECK is held against the request's `Literal` by `test_check_constraints.py` (`rollbackOrder` and `onGateFailure`), and `scripts/netconf_lab_runbook.py` and the four test modules that build the module's tables by hand create `lifecycle_subscription` too (registering an element looks for templates and a failed apply looks for subscribers).
- **Not done.** `MGT-15.8` (the software job as a real exchange with the element: it needs a vendor and an O1 software-management decision, `SB-10`) and `MGT-15.9` (the KPI gate, after `MGT-11`). `MGT-14.8` (placeholders and scope on a template). The shared library's mutation pilot covers `roles.py`, which gained one line of data (the `INTERNAL_ONLY` entry for the new route): the gateway's `INTERNAL_ONLY_CALLS` has the three methods on it, the same as the approval subscriptions beside it.
- **Verified here / only in CI.** Here: `ruff`, `mypy` (`scripts/run_mypy.sh ran-nf-oam gui-bff`), the `ran-nf-oam` suite (858, 22 new; coverage 98.1 against a floor of 97), `gui-bff` and the gateway's role tests, Postgres 16: the migration round trip (up, down one, up), `check_migration_matches_models.py` (it caught the two new columns declared on the wrong class once), the regenerated OpenAPI specs (only additions; `check_breaking_changes.py` finds no break) and the cross-service suite; the GUI: `tsc -b`, Vitest (284), `vite build`, `validate:diagrams`. Only CI: the two upgrade lanes (the previous release's code on the new schema, an upgrade that keeps its data), the contract test against a running stack, the Helm render, the browser check of the GUI (it does not open these tabs; `MGT-14.6a`) and a real receiver getting a notice.

### PR-SB-7 — a VES event receiver (SB-7.1 to 7.5); SB-7.6 and a try against a real sender open

Closed from `OPEN_ITEMS.md` (0.7.0 scope, row 6). An O1 adaptor reports faults, heartbeats and measurements as VES events; RAN NF OAM took them only on its own routes (`POST /alarms/ingest`, `/pm-reports`, the endpoint heartbeat), so an adaptor that speaks VES (ONAP style) needed a shim. Now it can post to `POST /ves/eventListener/v7` (and `/eventBatch`).

- **Where.** `ran-nf-oam/app/ves.py` has what has no database (the credential check, the header schema, the meaning of each domain: 100 % covered); the route and its apply step are in `main.py` and reuse the code of the routes they stand in for. To make that possible three bodies were lifted out of their routes, unchanged: `_raise_alarm` (`ingest_alarm`), `_accept_pm_report` (`receive_pm_report`) and `_record_heartbeat` (`endpoint_heartbeat`). A VES event and the same fact on the original route therefore cannot end up differently (the FM / PM service check, the PM subscription precondition, the severity values). Not a separate service: the receiver needs the same database and the same helpers, and the cost of a module (image, role, chart entry, route) would be larger than the thing.
- **SB-7.1, the schema.** A post is `{"event": {...}}` or `{"eventList": [...]}` (not both, not neither, at most 500: a 400 `VES_BAD_REQUEST`). Every event's `commonEventHeader` is checked against the VES 7.2 required members (`domain`, `eventId`, `eventName`, `lastEpochMicrosec`, `priority`, `reportingEntityName`, `sequence`, `sourceName`, `startEpochMicrosec`, `version`, `vesEventListenerVersion`) before anything is applied; the 400 names the members and the kind of error (`missing`, `literal_error`) and never a value the sender wrote. After that each event is applied on its own and the 202 answers `{events, applied, results[{index, domain, outcome, codes}]}` with `APPLIED`, `PARTIAL`, `IGNORED` or `REJECTED`. **A refused event is not a 4xx.** A VES sender resends what it is refused, so an event for an element we do not know would block its batch for ever; the per-event codes are `MANAGED_ENTITY_NOT_FOUND`, `O1_ENDPOINT_NOT_FOUND`, `SCHEMA_VALIDATION_FAILED` (no PM subscription), `O1_SERVICE_NOT_SUPPORTED`. Exception text is never returned.
- **SB-7.2, fault to alarm.** The element is `sourceName`. `eventSeverity` CRITICAL, MAJOR, MINOR, WARNING raise (lower-cased, as `/alarms/ingest` stores them); NORMAL clears. The identity of an alarm is element + `alarmCondition` (+ `alarmInterfaceA` when given): the same open alarm at the same severity is not raised again (`ALARM_ALREADY_OPEN`), at another severity it is updated (`ALARM_UPDATED`, `changed_at` set), NORMAL clears the open one as `PATCH /alarms/{id}/clear` does (`cleared`, `cleared_at`, `clear_user_id` `ves`) and does nothing when none is open (`NO_OPEN_ALARM`, still accepted). `probableCause` is the `alarmCondition`, `specificProblem` the event's. The alarm id is minted here as ever. **Decision:** repeated raises are merged. `/alarms/ingest` makes a row for every call; a VES sender repeats an active alarm on a timer (and on retries), and a row per repeat would bury the console. The price is that a sender that reuses one `alarmCondition` for different faults on one element sees them merged.
- **SB-7.3, heartbeat.** The element's O1 adaptor endpoint gets the heartbeat of `POST /o1-adaptor-endpoints/{id}/heartbeat` (the time, and DISCOVERED or DEGRADED becomes ACTIVE). An element with no endpoint is `REJECTED`.
- **SB-7.4, PM.** `measurement`: one PM report per `additionalMeasurements` entry (its `name` is the counter type; numeric fields in `arrayOfFields` (VES 7) or `hashMap` (VES 5, 6) are the values; the field `cellId` names the cell, otherwise the element; the timestamp is `lastEpochMicrosec`). `stndDefined` with `stndDefinedNamespace` `3GPP-PerformanceAssurance`: one report per `measInfoList` entry of `perf3gppFields` (the `sMeasInfoId` is the counter type, each `measObjInstId` a cell, results by position against `sMeasTypesList`; a `suspectFlag` true or a non-number is left out). Both go through `_accept_pm_report`, so a PM subscription on the counter is needed, as for `POST /pm-reports`. Other domains and namespaces are accepted and `IGNORED` (`DOMAIN_NOT_MAPPED`).
- **SB-7.5, who may post.** HTTP Basic. The password comes from `RAN_NF_OAM_VES_PASSWORD` or `RAN_NF_OAM_VES_PASSWORD_FILE` (`smo_shared.secretfile.read_secret`), the user from `RAN_NF_OAM_VES_USERNAME` (default `ves`). **Nothing changes for an existing deployment: without a password the listener does not exist (404 `VES_LISTENER_DISABLED`).** A password set twice, or a file that cannot be read, answers 503 with nothing of why (the log has it): it fails closed. The check runs as a dependency, before the body is parsed, so a wrong password gets 401 whatever the body (a body that is not JSON at all is rejected by the server before any route runs). Both halves of the credential are compared with `hmac.compare_digest` whatever the first said. The route is declared in the OpenAPI document with its own `vesBasicAuth` scheme, not the gateway's bearer token (the contract and role walks still hold for every other route).
- **Not behind R1.** An adaptor posts to the module directly, as the stub does for its own emits. Through the gateway the call carries a bearer token, which is not a Basic credential, so it is refused. **Deliberately not done:** (1) a `commandList` back-channel; (2) the other VES domains (`syslog`, `thresholdCrossingAlert`, `mobileFlow`, ...) and `stndDefined` fault supervision; (3) TLS client authentication of the sender; (4) a rate limit for a sender (an adaptor is a trusted party with a credential; the gateway's budget does not apply to it); (5) `SB-7.6`, a Kafka consumer: it needs the Kafka variant of the message bus (`MSG-3.4`), which does not exist. **Not verified:** the mapping is written from the VES 7.2 and 3GPP `perf3gppFields` schemas as the author knew them and exercised by the tests' own examples; no real sender has been tried (no ONAP-style sender and no sample files in `specs/`). `SB-7.7` in `OPEN_ITEMS.md` is that try.
- **Tests.** `ran-nf-oam/tests/test_ves.py` (52); `ves.py` 100 %. The new route is under the contract test (`tests_integration/test_contract_schemathesis.py`: with no password configured every generated input must answer a documented status).

### PR-MGT-2 — MSAC beyond writes (MGT-2.1 to 2.5)

Closed from `OPEN_ITEMS.md` (0.7.0 scope, row 6; `SA-RANOAM-1 (reach)`). TS 28.319 access rules (`msac.authorize`) decided who may write which managed object; reads and the other changes were not asked.

- **A switch, off by default.** `RAN_NF_OAM_MSAC_REACH` (`true`, `1`, `yes`, `on`). Off, nothing is asked and nothing changes: an Identity and Role made for writes (whose rules name `update`) would otherwise start refusing every read on upgrade. Turning it on is the operator's act and means adding `read` rules first. Making it the default is a later, announced change (`MGT-2.7`).
- **Who is asked, and by what name.** A caller whose invoker id (`smo_shared.invoker.invoker_id`: the id the gateway vouches for, or the rApp an SMO module acts for) is a registered MSAC Identity. A caller that is not an Identity, or did not come through the gateway, is not asked (as for writes). **Decision, differing from the writes:** a read takes no `msacRole`. On a write the requester names `requestedBy` and may add a role; for a read the only name is the invoker id, and a role the caller names itself would let a restricted Identity pick a wider one (the same weakness exists on writes and is recorded here, not changed: `requestedBy` and `msacRole` are self-declared there).
- **What each route needs.** `read` on the element, or the function when one is named: `GET .../config`, `GET .../config-history`, `.../config-history/diff` (not in the plan: they return the values the element had, which is what the config read returns), `POST /pm-subscriptions`, `POST /fm-subscriptions` (a subscription is the right to be sent the data), `GET /pm-files/{id}/file` (the file's element). `update` on the alarm's element and function: `PATCH /alarms/{id}/ack` and `/clear` (**plan said only "the same"; they change the record, so `update`**). `exec` on the element: `POST /software-management-jobs` (**the same decision: it runs a procedure**). `read` on `/` for `POST /file-subscriptions`, which is sent the ready-notice of every file: only a rule on `/*` selects the root, so an Identity allowed on part of the network cannot subscribe to all of it. `GET /files` leaves out the files of elements the caller may not read (a filtered list, as the scope of `PR-SEC-10` does, not a 403). The scope check comes first; MSAC is then asked. A denial is 403 `MSAC_ACCESS_DENIED` naming the caller, the operation and the target path (the path is built from the request's own reference).
- **Not covered, on purpose** (`MGT-2.6` is the decision; made later, see "PR-MGT-2 (follow-up)" below): the list routes other than `GET /files` (alarms, subscriptions, jobs, endpoints, KPIs, registries) have no single target; a rule per element cannot say what a list may show, and the scope filter exists for that; subscription deletes (quiet, idempotent, and the id is the capability); the adaptor-facing routes (ingest, PM and file reports, heartbeat, advance), which an adaptor and not an operator calls; DME and MLMR (no managed element).
- **Tests.** `ran-nf-oam/tests/test_msac_reach.py` (41). Not tested: a real gateway in front of an Identity (the tests set `X-R1-Invoker-Id` themselves).

### PR-SEC-10 (follow-up) — the rest of RAN NF OAM's reads, ownership of a job, the DME action list (SEC-10.9, SEC-10.11, SEC-10.7 in part)

Closed from `OPEN_ITEMS.md` (`SEC-10.9`, `SEC-10.11`; `SEC-10.7` narrowed). Decision: `docs/adr/0005-tenant-region-authorization.md` section 10. The rule of `smo_shared/scope.py` is unchanged (so is the mutation pilot: 1859 mutants, none touched); no migration.

- **What existed first (checked).** A route walk of RAN NF OAM's `GET` routes found, beyond the five groups the item named, three more that showed everything to a scoped caller: `GET /o1-adaptor-endpoints/{id}/host-keys`, `GET /rapp-approvals/{id}` (its docstring said "an rApp reads its own request by id"; the code did not check) and `GET /decision-records/{id}` (the list is internal-only at the gateway, the single read was not).
- **The managed-object tree and topology.** `managed_object.managed_element_ref` is the element, so the rule applies row by row (`scoped_to_elements`). By DN: **404 `MANAGED_OBJECT_NOT_FOUND`, identical to a DN that is not in the tree.** The task text said "403 by reference"; a DN is an object name, and the ADR's own reasoning (an unregistered reference is refused exactly as an out-of-scope one, so the answer does not say which exist) cannot be met by 403 for a hidden node beside 404 for a missing one, so it is the 404 of "as if it did not exist". `children` and `subtree` filter every level (`mo_tree.subtree(..., restrict)`); `POST .../managed-objects/refresh` and the cell-guard writes name an element: 403. `GET /topology` filters its query; **`GET /topology/links` restricts the set of elements the links are computed from** (`topology.cell_links(..., restrict)`): a neighbour declared on an element outside the scope becomes `EXTERNAL` (the existing word for "no element I know declares it"), `AMBIGUOUS` and `reciprocal` count visible elements only, and the answer never names a hidden element. Filtering the finished list would have left the element in `bElement`.
- **KPI schedules.** The schedule's `managed_element_ref` is nullable: null is the whole network. Shown (list, by id, delete, replace) to a scoped caller only when it names an element inside the scope; `PUT` of an outside element, of none, or over an id that exists and is hidden is 403. The worker that runs schedules is not a caller. Internal-only at the gateway for `PUT`/`DELETE`, so this is defence in depth for the writes and the real change for the reads.
- **File subscriptions.** No list route exists. The subscription is sent every file's `notifyFileReady`, so a scoped caller gets **403** on create and **204 with nothing removed** on delete. Not taken: storing the claim on the subscription and filtering deliveries (a column, hence revision `0035`, and a per-file lookup in `POST /pm-files`); an rApp cannot create one through the gateway today, so the cheaper rule loses nothing real. If a deployment wants scoped file subscriptions, that is the follow-up.
- **The registries.** No element, region or tenant on `vendor_capability` or `cm_schema_cache`. **Derived rule (a decision for the owner):** a vendor's entry is shown to a scoped caller when one of the elements inside its scope has that `vendor_name`; `GET /capabilities` is computed over those vendors; a loaded CM schema when such an entry names it (own or spec); bundled schemas (3GPP, O-RAN) to everyone. 404 for a hidden vendor or schema, the same as an absent one. Rejected: a region and tenant column (the data-model decision `SEC-10.7` waits for) and hiding the registries from every scoped caller.
- **Ownership of a job (`SEC-10.11`).** `write_config_job.invoker_id` (stored since `AI-10.2`) is the owner. A rollback job and the automatic revert are made without an invoker (so an undo never counts against the rApp's hourly budget; setting it would have changed that), so ownership follows `rollback_of`: `scoping.owned_jobs` is a recursive query (rollbacks of rollbacks included), `job_owned_by` the same walk for one job. Applies to `GET /config-jobs/{id}`, the list, `POST /config-jobs/{id}/rollback` (404 before the scope is asked, so a non-owner cannot tell whether the job exists), `GET /rapp-approvals/{id}` and `GET /decision-records/{id}`. **Who is held to it: a caller with a scope claim that is an rApp or an SMO module acting for one.** The task said nothing may change for an unscoped caller and also that an rApp may read only its own jobs; both hold only if ownership follows the claim, so an upgrade changes nothing until a claim is set (as for the scope). Widening it to every rApp is one line (`_job_owner_filter` in `ran-nf-oam/app/main.py`) and changes what an unscoped rApp may read: the owner's decision. "Or its instance": an instance has one invoker; a second invoker that an rApp registered for itself is another owner (RAN NF OAM cannot ask rApp Management). Two existing tests in `test_scope.py` made their job through an operator and read or rolled it back as a scoped rApp, which is exactly what is now a 404; they now make the job through the same rApp with a wider claim and keep every assertion about the scope. A refused rollback is not recorded as a safeguard refusal (a new code in the vocabulary of `safeguard-subscriptions` and the GUI). Config history still shows the job ids of other rApps' writes to an element the caller may read: it is the element's history.
- **DME `GET /actions` and `/actions/{id}` (`SEC-10.7`, in part).** `dme_action_record.managed_element_ref` is the first change's element; every change names its own. DME may not read `managed_entity` (module isolation, `tests_integration/test_table_owners.py`) and has no place of its own, so `_visible_elements` pages through `GET /ran-nf-oam/managed-entities?limit=500`, where `R1Client` has passed the rApp's claim on; an action is shown when it names at least one element and all are in that set. Fail closed twice: the call is made only if the claim travelled (`get_originator_scope() == scope`; an rApp request with no invoker id would be read by RAN NF OAM as unscoped and answered with every element), and a non-200 is a 502. An action that names no element is hidden from a scoped caller (it has no place to be inside). An SMO module that was itself given a claim (not acting for an rApp) sees nothing: DME cannot pass its claim on. **What stays out, and why** is in the ADR and `dme/README.md`: no other DME or MLMR datum has an element, region or tenant.
- **Not done.** `SEC-10.10` (GUI forms to edit a claim or an element's place) and `SEC-10.12` (scoping the console's users, `GUI-5.1`): not trivial, left open. `SEC-10.8` (OPA).
- **Tests.** `ran-nf-oam/tests/test_scope_reads.py` (23, including a walk that fails for any read route not classified as about elements or not, and shows a claim that matches nothing nothing of the seeded data on every route about elements), `dme/tests/test_scope.py` (6), two tests in `tests_integration/test_tenant_region_scope.py`. Near misses covered: a damaged claim, a claim with no invoker, an SMO module with its own claim, an id that exists and is hidden, a rollback two deep, a stray node of another element under an allowed parent.
- **Verified here / only in CI.** Here: `ran-nf-oam`, `dme`, the cross-service suite with Postgres 16, `scripts/migrate.py` and `check_migration_matches_models.py` (138 tables), the whole `tests_integration` suite (623 passed, 43 skipped for tools or a stack that is not here; it holds the contract tests and the OpenAPI check), OpenAPI regenerated (`dme.json`, `ran-nf-oam.json`: descriptions only), coverage (`ran-nf-oam` 98.1 against 97, `dme` 98.4 against 98), `ruff`, `mypy` for both modules. The mutation pilot was not run: `smo_shared` is untouched. Only CI: Postgres 18, the previous-release lanes (no revision, so they do not run), the stack with a real gateway in front of a scoped rApp.

### PR-MGT-2 (follow-up) — MSAC on the list routes and subscription deletes (MGT-2.6)

Closed from `OPEN_ITEMS.md`. The decision asked for in the item, made with the owner: **the model is the scope filter of `PR-SEC-10`**, behind the existing switch (`RAN_NF_OAM_MSAC_REACH`, off by default; with it off, and for a caller that is not a registered Identity, nothing changes).

- **Lists.** A list route leaves out the rows whose element the caller may not `read` (`msac.readable`, `msac.unreadable_elements`; the target of a row is its element, `/ManagedElement=X`, like `GET /files` since `MGT-2.5`, so a rule on a function alone does not show the element's rows; function-level rows would need a query per alarm source). Routes: alarms, PM and FM subscriptions, software jobs, config jobs (shown only when every element the job wrote to is readable), O1 endpoints, managed elements, cell guards, the topology export and links (links are worked out among the readable elements, as for the scope), and the children and subtree of a node. `GET /kpis/{name}` is computed over the performance files of readable elements (`kpi.compute(..., exclude=)`). A read of one node of the tree by DN needs `read` on its element: 403 `MSAC_ACCESS_DENIED`, asked after the scope (404). `total` counts what is shown.
- **Subscription deletes.** Idempotent and quiet, so the answer stays 204: a PM or FM subscription is removed only by a caller that may `read` its element (the right that created it, `MGT-2.2`); a file subscription only by one that may `read` the whole network (`MGT-2.5`). Otherwise nothing is removed. `delete` was not used as the operation: MSAC's `delete` means deleting a managed object.
- **Not covered, and why.** The registries and the KPI schedules (no single target; a schedule with no element is the whole network), the software-campaign and element-onboarding lists (already scoped; not named by the item), the adaptor-facing routes (unchanged). `MGT-2.7` (making the switch the default) stays for a release after 0.7.0.
- **Shape.** The switch is read in `msac.reach_on()` (it moved from `main.py`; `docs/CONFIGURATION.md` regenerated). Tests: `ran-nf-oam/tests/test_msac_lists.py` (27). Not proved: a real gateway in front of an Identity (the tests set the invoker header themselves), as for `MGT-2.1` to `2.5`.

### PR-SB-10 — a vendor profile on the O1 stub, and a first profile that is a stand-in (SB-10.1 to 10.4 for `example-du`; SB-10.5, the real one, open)

Closed from `OPEN_ITEMS.md` (0.7.0 scope, row 6), **in part, and the part that is closed is the mechanism**. `PR-SB-10` says it needs a vendor simulator or lab. None was available, and a vendor's YANG is not something to write from memory (the same reason `PR-SB-4` stayed blocked). So the work is: define what a profile is, build everything around it and prove it on an invented vendor, so that the real one is a directory of data.

- **A profile is a directory under `mock-o1-adaptor/app/profiles/<name>/`** (under `app/` so it travels in the image, which copies only `app/`): `profile.json` (vendor name, `supportedServices`, `supportedVendorModes`, `conformanceMode`, the schema it brings, `iocDefaults`), `yang/`, `schema/<name>.json` (the descriptor `scripts/ingest_yang_schema.py` generates from the YANG), `README.md` (the deviations: SB-10.3) and `conformance-report.md` (SB-10.4). `app/profile.py` loads and checks it (a name is a directory name, never a path: `../x` is refused; every malformed profile is refused with the reason) and builds the body of `POST /ran-nf-oam/vendor-onboarding` from it, so the capability entry (SB-10.2) and the stub come from one file.
- **`MOCK_O1_PROFILE=<name>`** makes the stub that vendor: `GET /capabilities` declares it, `get-config` of its classes returns its defaults on top of the generic ones, and a vendor that does not declare `O1_RESTCONF` has no RESTCONF root (404 on `/.well-known/host-meta` and `/restconf/...`). The three existing `MOCK_O1_*` variables still win. Unset, the stub is exactly what it was; a name that is not a profile stops it at start. The stub does **not** validate writes against the vendor's YANG: RAN NF OAM does that before dispatch (`schema_problems`), which is where the deviations are enforced and where the registry entry matters.
- **`example-du`, the stand-in.** Vendor `example-vendor`; NETCONF only; services PROV, FM, PM, FILE, HEARTBEAT (no software management); `COMBINED` with TS 28.541. Its YANG (`example-du-ext.yang`, self-contained) adds `exampleTxBackoffDb` (0..20) and `exampleBeamMode` to `NRCellDU`, narrows `NRSectorCarrier.configuredMaxTxPower` to 0..40 dBm, and adds the class `ExampleBeamProfile`. Its README lists these five deviations and what each does at RAN NF OAM. **It is invented.** Everything it shows is true of the mechanism; nothing in it is true of any vendor.
- **SB-10.4, the report.** `conformance-report.md` is the kit run against the stub in the profile, RAN NF OAM read back, NETCONF only: 23 pass, 0 fail, 13 skipped (the ten RESTCONF checks because the vendor does not declare it, the three software checks because it does not offer SWM). `tests_integration/test_vendor_profile.py` regenerates it on every run and fails if it differs (`SMO_UPDATE_PROFILE_REPORT=1` rewrites it), so it cannot go stale; the report format has no timings or run ids, so it is deterministic.
- **What is checked.** Unit (`mock-o1-adaptor/tests/test_profile.py`, 24): the loader, a descriptor equal to what the YANG gives (editing the YANG without regenerating fails), nothing changing without the variable, the declaration, the stub's overrides, no RESTCONF root, defaults, a bad name, the kit passing. Integration (`tests_integration/test_vendor_profile.py`, 7), against the real RAN NF OAM app in the mesh: onboarding and the registry entry equal to the declaration, onboarding twice, discovery from a registered element, the five deviations enforced on a write (43 dBm refused, 40 sent and read back through the stub; an attribute out of range, an unknown enum value, the vendor's own class), a RESTCONF element refused for this vendor, a software job refused, and the report.
- **Not known** (and said in the profile's README): whether a real vendor's YANG is as plain as this (the reader does not evaluate `must`, `when`, `if-feature` or `deviation`); a real vendor's NETCONF behaviour; its VES; how its `get-config` spells values; scale and timing. **Open:** `SB-10.5`, the real profile.

### PR-SEC-2.4 — Postgres over TLS, and every client verifying it (SEC-2.4)

Closed from `OPEN_ITEMS.md`. Opt in, like the rest of PR-SEC-2: the default stack and the default chart render are byte for byte what they were (`helm template` of the old and the new chart differ only in the random passwords the chart draws).

- **The certificate (`scripts/mtls_certs.py`).** `init` now also makes `DIR/postgres/{tls.crt,tls.key,ca.crt}`: a server certificate (serverAuth and clientAuth, names `postgres`, `localhost`, 127.0.0.1, ::1 and `SMO_MTLS_NAMES`) and the CA bundle the clients check it against (during a CA rotation, old and new). It is a separate tuple (`DATABASES`) that is neither in `SERVERS` nor in `SERVICES`, because the mTLS overlay and its tests enumerate those and Postgres takes no part in that overlay: it is verified by its clients, it does not ask them for a certificate. `renew` and `status` already walk every directory with a `tls.crt`, so rotation and expiry cover it.
- **Compose (`docker-compose.pgtls.yml`, `pgtls/pg_hba.conf`).** The overlay replaces Postgres's command (keeping `log_min_duration_statement`) with a shell line that, as root, copies the certificate and key to `/run/pgtls` owned by `postgres` with the key at 0600 (Postgres refuses a key anyone else can read, and the bind-mounted files are 0644), then `exec docker-entrypoint.sh postgres -c ssl=on -c ssl_cert_file=... -c ssl_key_file=... -c ssl_min_protocol_version=TLSv1.2 -c hba_file=/etc/smo/pg_hba.conf`. The hba file is `local trust` (the unix socket of `pg_isready` and the image's init scripts), `hostssl scram-sha-256` and `host reject`: a plain connection from the network is refused by the server, not just discouraged. Every service that opens a database connection (23: the modules, the two workers, the four sample rApps, `migrate` and `db-backup`) gets `PGSSLMODE=${SMO_DB_SSLMODE:-verify-full}` and `PGSSLROOTCERT=/run/pgtls/ca.crt`, the bundle mounted read-only. The URLs in `docker-compose.yml` are not touched: libpq reads both variables from the environment, so SQLAlchemy and psycopg verify the chain and the name `postgres` without a code change. A test lists the services with `SMO_DATABASE_URL` in the base file and fails if the overlay misses one, if any default weakens (`disable`, `allow`, `prefer`, `require`), or if the overlay puts a URL in. `scripts/pg_env.sh` (the backup and restore scripts) now also takes `sslrootcert` from the URL and leaves `PGSSLMODE` / `PGSSLROOTCERT` of the environment alone.
- **PgBouncer.** `pgbouncer/entrypoint.sh` takes `PGBOUNCER_SERVER_TLS_SSLMODE` (default `prefer`, as before) and `PGBOUNCER_SERVER_TLS_CA_FILE`; `verify-ca` and `verify-full` without a CA file stop the container at start with a message. The overlay sets `verify-full` and the CA for the pooler's connection to Postgres. The services' connection to the pooler stays plain (the pooler serves no TLS to its clients: not taken), so with `--profile pooler` the services need `SMO_DB_SSLMODE=disable` and only the pooler-to-Postgres leg is verified; the overlay says so.
- **The chart (`postgres.tls`).** `enabled` (default false), `secretName` (`postgres-tls`: keys `tls.crt`, `tls.key`, `ca.crt`, what cert-manager writes), `certManager.{enabled,issuerRef,duration,renewBefore}`. On, the StatefulSet gets `args` (`ssl=on`, the files under `/run/pg-tls`, TLS 1.2 minimum, `hba_file` from the ConfigMap `postgres-hba`, whose rules a test compares with `pgtls/pg_hba.conf`), the Secret mounted with `defaultMode: 0440` (owned by root, group `fsGroup: 70`: the case Postgres accepts) and, with `certManager.enabled`, a `Certificate` for `postgres`, `postgres.<ns>.svc`, `postgres.<ns>.svc.cluster.local` and `localhost` from the issuer named in `issuerRef` (required: the render fails without it; with `mtls.certManager.createCA` that is `smo-mtls-ca`). `smo.databaseUrl` then ends in `?sslmode=verify-full&sslrootcert=/run/pg-tls/ca.crt` for every module and the migrate Job, and each of them (and the `wait-for-schema` init containers) mounts only the `ca.crt` item of that Secret at `/run/pg-tls`, so a client never holds the server's key. With the bundled Postgres off, `postgres.tls` changes nothing (the external one keeps its own `sslmode`). The template guards keep the default render identical.
- **Proved.** `scripts/pg_tls_check.py` asks four things of a server: the database's CA connects and `pg_stat_ssl` says the session is encrypted; **a CA that did not sign the certificate fails** (a made-up one, `certificate verify failed`); the right CA with another host name fails (`hostaddr` keeps the connection on the same server; the certificate does not name the other); and, where the server serves `hostssl` only, `sslmode=disable` is refused (`pg_hba.conf rejects connection`). `--spawn` starts a throwaway Postgres (initdb and postgres from the system, as an unprivileged user when run as root) with exactly the overlay's settings and runs the four: all four pass here, and `tests_integration/test_pg_tls.py` does it as a test (skipped where there is no `initdb`). The whole path of the services was also run here (below, PR-SEC-5.4b): six services as processes against that TLS Postgres with `PGSSLMODE=verify-full`, `migrate.py` and `db_roles.py` included; the server counted 5 sessions over TLS and none plain. The chart render tests ran with `helm` 3.16 (built from source here) and pass; `helm lint` is clean.
- **Only CI can show.** That the postgres image's entrypoint takes the overlay's command and starts with the certificate (`compose-pgtls` in `smo-tests.yml`: every service healthy, every session encrypted, the four checks against the container, a service given the CA of an unrelated PKI refusing to connect); that the pods start on a cert-manager Secret and that the kubelet's `0440` and `fsGroup` give Postgres a key it accepts (not run: needs a cluster and cert-manager); PgBouncer's `server_tls_sslmode=verify-full` with Alpine's build; the effect on the backup scripts of `PGSSLROOTCERT`.
- **Not taken.** TLS to the pooler's clients; client certificates for the database (the role passwords stay the credential; `hostssl ... cert` would add a second factor and a certificate per service); switching TLS on in a release that already runs (the pre-upgrade migrate Job connects with `verify-full` to a Postgres that still serves plain, so the upgrade stops there with the old pods serving; the way is a new install with a restore, or a hand-made step in between); a CA bundle for an external Postgres in the pods (`postgres.external.sslmode: verify-full` uses the image's system store; a private CA there is `sslrootcert` through a mounted file and is the operator's); making TLS the default for the bundled database (the upgrade case above is why).

### PR-SEC-5.4b — the introspection cache measured (SEC-5.4); the default recommended, not applied (SEC-5.5)

Closes `SEC-5.4`. `SEC-5.5` stays open as the owner's decision, with the numbers and a recommendation.

- **The measurement tooling.** `scripts/load_run.py` now reads, at the start and at the end of the measured part, `smo_http_requests_total{route="/oauth2/introspect"}` at SME and the gateway's `smo_introspection_cache_total` and its own request count (every route but `/metrics`, `/live`, `/ready`, `/health`, `/bootstrap`), and reports `introspection` in `load-results.json` and a sentence in the table: the introspections SME answered, the calls through the gateway (counted by the gateway over the same two readings, so callers still in flight at the first reading skew the ratio by at most their number over the calls), the share per 100 calls and the cache's hit ratio. A page that cannot be read leaves the part out; it never fails the load. `scripts/introspection_compare.py` sets a run without the cache beside one with it and **fails** when either run has an error, when the run without the cache did not cost about one introspection per call (it was not what it says), when the run with it counted no cache lookups, or when the cache cut the introspections by less than half. It reports; it loosens nothing.
- **The compose lane.** `smo-load.yml` runs the main load as before (its sizing samples are the first run's), then recreates only the gateway with `R1_INTROSPECTION_CACHE_SECONDS=30`, checks the variable is in the container, runs the same load again (no new seed) into `load-out/cache-30` and runs the comparison; the table goes to the job summary and the artifact. The workflow runs on a pull request that changes it, so this pull request's own CI gives the second reading.
- **The first reading, without Docker (`scripts/load_local.py`).** A Postgres over TLS (the PR-SEC-2.4 settings) and SME, DME, Onboarding, rApp Management, RAN NF OAM and the gateway as processes on one 4-core machine that also runs the load generator; `verify-full` for every database connection; 8 callers in flight for 45 s after a 5 s warm-up, the same weighted mix of eight gateway routes and the token route, one rApp token. The run was made twice with the same result:

  | | introspections at SME | calls through the gateway | per 100 calls | req/s | p50 ms | p95 ms | p99 ms | errors |
  |---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
  | cache off | 370 | 376 | 98.4 | 8.6 | 950 | 1242 | 1316 | 0 |
  | cache 30 s | 6 | 673 | 0.9 | 15.5 | 522 | 688 | 739 | 0 |

  (the first run: 355 for 358 calls, 99.2 per 100, against 3 for 713, 0.4 per 100, at 8.2 and 16.4 req/s.) SME answered 99 % fewer introspections, and because each introspection is a database round trip behind a thread pool the gateway's throughput nearly doubled and its latency halved on that machine. **What this does not say.** The absolute latencies are those of a small machine running everything; one token is the best case for the cache (a fleet with one token per rApp and a call every few seconds per token saves `1 - 1/(calls per token per 30 s)` of its checks, not 99 %); the second run follows the first on a warm database, so the latency gain is partly that; sizing from it would be wrong. The number to trust is the one the code determines: without the cache one introspection per call, with it one per token per TTL.
- **The default (SEC-5.5): recommended 30 s, not applied.** 30 s is short against a one-hour token and halves the work of SME several times over; the cost is that a token whose invoker was offboarded or whose role or scope claim changed is honoured for up to 30 s more by every gateway replica except the one that carried the change (`r1-termination/README.md`, "Introspection cache"). That is a change in what an upgrade does to revocation for every installation, measured once on one machine; applying it without the owner would be choosing a security trade for them. If it is applied, the places are `R1_INTROSPECTION_CACHE_SECONDS` in `docker-compose.yml` and `modules.r1-termination.env` in the chart's `values.yaml`, and the README paragraph. A revocation broadcast between replicas (for an immediate effect everywhere) is not built: with one replica per revocation path it adds nothing, and `LISTEN/NOTIFY` or a shared store costs the round trip the cache exists to remove.
- **Tests.** `tests_integration/test_introspection_load.py` (11): the Prometheus parsing, the summary arithmetic and its fallback, and the comparison's verdict on a good run and on seven runs that do not show what they say.

### PR-GUI-8c — the browser check of the declared rApp pages (GUI-8.7)

Closes the last step of `PR-GUI-8`. `PR-GUI-8b` had no job for it because neither Docker nor a browser could be used where it was written; this adds both halves and has been run here against the real built GUI.

- **`scripts/gui_rapp_stub.py`.** A stand-in rApp (standard library only) that is also the package host: `/writable.csar` is the committed Energy Saving package, whose manifest carries an `operatorUi`; `/readonly.csar` is the same package renamed `EnergySaving_rApp_ReadOnly` (in the manifest and in the ASD, which is where Onboarding reads the name) with its page replaced by a small `readOnly: true` one. It serves the routes of the Energy Saving declaration with two canned cells, keeps the override in memory so a click changes the next read, and lists the calls it received at `/_calls`. `setup` onboards both packages from its own URL, waits for `AVAILABLE` and creates an instance of each with `operatorApiBase` set, as the sample `demo.py` does.
- **`scripts/gui_rapp_pages_e2e.py`.** Chromium driven by Playwright, as `gui_e2e.py` is. As the operator: the directory lists both instances and marks both as declaring a page; a search narrows it to the one it names; a row opens `/rapps/<instance>`; the page draws its three panels with the stub's data and a sparkline per row; the row drawer draws its chart and JSON and fetches its history table for the open row; the button's click is answered with the declared success text; the row action asks the browser's confirm, reaches the rApp **with the signed-in user in the body**, and the table is read again (the other action appears); a hand-made call to an undeclared route, with another instance's id in the path, is refused `UNDECLARED_ROUTE`, and a change on the read-only declaration is refused `RAPP_READ_ONLY`; no call of the browser names the rApp's address; a pin shows in the sidebar and survives a reload; `axe-core` finds no serious or critical WCAG 2 A/AA violation on the page. As the viewer: the same panels and data, no change button and the sentence saying why, a hand-made change refused `FORBIDDEN`, a hand-made read of a declared route answered, an undeclared route refused. 31 checks; exit 0 only when all pass.
- **The job.** A step of `smo-gui-e2e.yml`'s `gui` job, before the step that restarts the BFF with a 20 s session: the CSAR and the stub are copied into `r1-termination`'s `/srv/scratch` (as the runbook does for its package server), the stub is started there, `setup` onboards and prints the two instance ids, and the check runs on the runner. The GUI was built before the packages existed, which is the point of the step (a page "with no GUI rebuild"). Its screenshots go with the existing artifact. The workflow now also runs on a change to the two scripts and to the Energy Saving package.
- **Run here.** Everything but Docker: the built GUI (`vite preview`), the real GUI backend, SME, NFO, DME, Onboarding, rApp Management, RAN NF OAM and the gateway as processes, the TLS Postgres of PR-SEC-2.4, the stub on the machine's own address (a loopback address would be refused by the guard on `operatorApiBase`), and a headless Chromium unpacked from `@sparticuz/chromium` (Playwright's own download is blocked here). Result: the setup onboarded and created both instances, and the check passed 31 of 31, exit 0. The check counts only failures of the calls these pages make (`/api/rapps`, pins, rApp Management, Onboarding); the other pages' calls are `gui_e2e.py`'s, and without the modules this run did not start (AIMgF, FOCOM) the sidebar's own polling answered 502.
